A Well-Defined Jellyfish Algorithm for the Affine E 7 E_{7} Subfactor Planar Algebra
Melody Molander

Abstract
In this paper, we contribute to the Kuperberg program by giving a diagrammatic presentation of generators and relations for the affine E 7 E_{7} unshaded subfactor planar algebra. Using this presentation, we prove that its jellyfish algorithm is a well-defined surjection onto ℂ \mathbb{C} . In particular, this shows that the jellyfish algorithm is an invariant on closed diagrams for this planar algebra.



1. Introduction

Subfactor planar algebras were introduced by Vaughan Jones
[
13
]
as a diagrammatic version of the standard invariant for subfactors. Subfactors are unital inclusions of factors, i.e., von Neumann algebras with trivial center. We will assume all subfactors are irreducible and all factors of type
II_{1}
.

There are three main invariants of subfactors: a real number called the index, a principal graph, and the standard invariant (a unitary 2-category). In a landmark paper, Jones
[
11
]
proved that the set of indices for subfactors equals
\{4\cos^{2}(\pi/n):n\geq 3\}\cup[4,\infty]
. Subfactor classification has been the work of many authors and is completed up to index
5.25
. See
[
12
,
1
]
for the history of this classification program. The standard invariant can be axiomatizated in many ways other than as a 2-category or a subfactor planar algebra. This includes Popa’s
\lambda
-lattices
[
26
]
and Ocneanu’s paragroups for finite depth subfactors
[
23
,
8
]
.

A planar algebra is a collection of vector spaces along with an action of the planar operad. When the planar algebra is evaluable, spherical, and positive definite, it is said to be “subfactor". Given a subfactor planar algebra, Popa
[
26
]
proved, using the language of
\lambda
-lattices, that there is subfactor whose standard invariant is the planar algebra. On the other hand, given a subfactor with finite index, Jones proved that its standard invariant forms a subfactor planar algebra. The planar algebra language was found to be incredibly useful and pushed the subfactor classification program forward
[
3
,
10
,
24
]
. In particular,
[
3
]
proved that the extended Haagerup graph is realizable as a principal graph of a subfactor.

Constructions of subfactor planar algebras are typically made using generators and relations presentations
[
4
,
17
,
20
,
24
]
. When a planar algebra is a standard invariant of a subfactor, its diagrams have checkerboard shading. Dropping the references to shading in the definition of planar algebra yields an
unshaded subfactor planar algebra
, which is still a mathematically rich object. From presentations of unshaded subfactor planar algebras, one can obtain diagrammatics for fusion categories
[
17
]
and construct link invariants
[
21
]
. Morrison, Peters, and Snyder
[
20
]
posed the following program to construct and explore diagrammatic presentations:

The Kuperberg Program:
Provide a generators-and-relations presentation for every interesting planar algebra. Using this presentation, understand other properties of the associated categories, the planar algebra, and, in the subfactor case, the standard invariant.

Much progress has been made towards this program. The Kuperberg program for index less than 4 has been completed
[
20
,
4
]
. For index greater than 4, this has been ongoing research
[
24
,
3
,
10
,
19
]
. In this paper, we focus on giving a presentation by generators and relations of the unshaded index 4 subfactor planar algebra with principal graph affine
E_{7}
.

Index 4 subfactor planar algebras were classified by Popa
[
25
]
. Their principal graphs are necessarily the bipartite affine
ADE
Dynkin diagrams or the
A_{\infty}
Dynkin diagram. In the unshaded subfactor planar algebra case, while these do not give standard invariants of subfactors, there are further index 4 principal graphs beyond those found by Popa. For example, this author
[
17
]
proved that unshaded subfactor planar algebras can arise from all affine
A
Dynkin diagrams (not just the bipartite ones). The Kuperberg program at index 4 for the principal graph affine
A
was completed
[
17
]
and her thesis
[
18
]
includes the affine
D
case. The
A_{\infty}
subfactor planar algebras are the ubiquitous Temperley-Lieb planar algebras.

When describing a planar algebra using generators and relations, the
k
th vector space can be described as linear combinations of planar diagrams using the generators and strands with
k
strands on both the top and bottom boundary, modulo the skein relations. In order to show a planar algebra is “subfactor", often the toughest criteria to check is that its
0
th vector space of closed diagrams is one-dimensional. Bigelow, Morrison, Peters, and Snyder’s paper
[
3
]
introduced a novel evaluation algorithm called
the jellyfish algorithm
to show that this vector space was at most one-dimensional for the extended Haagerup subfactor planar algebra. Their argument also proved that this algorithm works for other planar algebras with sufficiently nice relations. Bigelow and Penneys
[
2
]
then gave criteria for which subfactor planar algebras of index greater than 4 can be constructed using the jellyfish algorithm.

The jellyfish algorithm was an extremely clever evaluation algorithm. In quantum topology, typically evaluation algorithms reduce the “common sense" complexity in each step. Examples include the Kauffman bracket which reduces the number of crossings of a link in every step, and the HOMFLYPT polynomial which reduces the number of crossings or the unknotting number in each step. However, the jellyfish algorithm may initially
increase
complexity! Specifically, the first steps may drastically increase the number of generators in a diagram. Despite being counterintuitive, this initial increase in complexity puts the elements of the vector space in a nice form that can then be simplified through a graph theory argument.

In this paper, we show that the jellyfish algorithm can be used to bound this vector space dimension from below as well for the affine
E_{7}
unshaded subfactor planar algebra. Thus, the jellyfish algorithm is an invariant on the set of closed diagrams for this planar algebra. In particular, we show that the algorithm gives a well-defined surjection onto
\mathbb{C}
, despite the numerous choices made throughout the algorithm’s process. Therefore, the vector space is at least one-dimensional.

In
[
3
]
, the authors show the vector space of closed diagrams is at least one-dimensional by locating it inside the graph planar algebra of the extended Haagerup principal graph. They first use numerical approximation methods to find the generator of the planar algebra inside of a 148375-dimensional vector space. Then to show that this generator satisfied the required relations, they use computer-assisted proofs using Mathematica.

Instead of embedding the planar algebra into the graph planar algebra, one can prove this vector space is at least one-dimensional by showing that the jellyfish algorithm gives a well-defined surjection. The relations of the affine
E_{7}
subfactor planar algebra allow generators to pass through strands as in a braided tensor category, which is not a relation afforded to the extended Haagerup planar algebra. Consequently, the complexity of the generators does not initially increase in the algorithm, but the number of crossings does. Using the braided skein theory, we can avoid any computer-assisted proofs that would have been used by embedding into the graph planar algebra. We hope these techniques can be generalized to get invariants of closed diagrams for other subfactor planar algebras or tensor categories with sufficiently nice braided structure.

In section
2
, we first review the necessary background material, starting with planar algebras, then the principal graph, and concluding with an overview of the jellyfish algorithm. Then we prove results that hold for any index 4 unshaded subfactor planar algebra in section
3
that will be heavily used in the subsequent sections. Section
4
first defines a planar algebra using generators and relations then shows that these relations are sufficient to define the affine
E_{7}
unshaded subfactor planar algebra (Theorem
4.1
). To do so, we define the jellyfish algorithm
4.4
and show it is a well-defined surjective function onto
\mathbb{C}
. The proof of this is substantial and is carried out across many subsections. The section then finishes with describing the minimal projections in the planar algebra. The final section
5
shows that if one starts with the unshaded subfactor planar algebra of affine
E_{7}
, it has a presentation that is necessarily the same as the one found in the previous section.

1.1
Acknowledgments

The author thanks Stephen Bigelow and David Penneys for their helpful conversations and insight.

2. Background

2.1
Planar Algebras

We first give an overview of planar algebras. The interested reader can find more background
[
17
,
18
,
20
,
24
]
. Let
X
denote a black, unoriented strand. A
planar tangle
is a diagram of a marked square with a (possibly empty) finite collection of marked squares interior to it connected by nonintersecting strands, where the number of strands on the top and bottom of each square must be equal (see figure
1
). Label the interior squares
D_{k}
and the exterior square
\mathcal{D}
. Let
\partial{D_{k}}
(respectively
\partial\mathcal{D}
) equal the number of strands ending on the top boundary of
D_{k}
(respectively
\mathcal{D}
).

Figure 1
:
An example of a planar tangle.

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be two planar tangles. Suppose for some interior square,
D_{k}^{1}
of
T_{1}
,
\partial D_{k}^{1}
equals
\partial\mathcal{D}^{2}
, the boundary of the exterior square of
T_{2}
. Then we can compose
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
by inserting
T_{2}
into the square
D_{k}^{1}
. See figure
2
for an example.

Figure 2
:
Composition of planar tangles.

Let
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
be a collection of vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
. For a square in a planar tangle with
k
boundary points on the top and bottom, associate it to the vector space
\mathcal{P}_{k}
. In figure
1
, the bottom interior square and the outside square are associated to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and the top interior square is associated to
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. To every planar tangle with interior squares
\{D_{k}\}
and exterior square
\mathcal{D}
, we associate a multilinear map
Z_{T}:\otimes_{k}\mathcal{P}_{\partial D_{k}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\partial\mathcal{D}}
.

We will use several common tangles in this paper, which are given in figure
3
. Many of these maps have subscripts dropped. In those cases, it should be clear from context the vector spaces in the domain and codomain. For both the right trace tangle and right partial trace tangle, there is an analogous left version, denoted
\text{tr}^{\ell}
and
E_{k}^{\ell}
, respectively.

…
…

(a)

The
identity tangle
,
I_{k}
, which associates to
Z_{I_{k}}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

(b)

The
multiplication tangle
,
M_{k}
, which associates to
Z_{M_{k}}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

…
k
…
k
…
\ell
…
\ell

(c)

The
tensor tangle
which associates to a map
\otimes:\mathcal{P}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\to\mathcal{P}_{k+\ell}
.

...
k

(d)

The
(right) trace tangle
which associates to a map
\text{tr}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

(e)

The
click (Frobenius) tangle
which associates to a map
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}
. This "clicks" the star to the clockwise region.

(f)

The
dual tangle
which associates to a map
\overline{\cdot}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}
. This is rotation by
\pi
.

(g)

The
(right) partial trace tangle
, which associates to a map
E_{k}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k-1}
.

(h)

The
inclusion tangle
which associates to a map
\iota_{k}:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
.

Figure 3
:
A collection of common planar tangles.

Planar algebras were first defined by Jones in
[
13
]
. A
planar algebra
is a collection of vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
,
\{\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},...\}
, along with for every planar tangle
T
, an associated a multilinear map
Z_{T}:\otimes_{k}\mathcal{P}_{\partial D_{k}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\partial\mathcal{D}}
such that

(i)

planar isotopic tangles give the same map;

(ii)

composition of tangles corresponds to the composition of their maps;

(iii)

for every
k\geq 0
, the identity tangle is the identity on
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

We frequently call the
k
th vector space
\mathcal{P}_{k}
of a planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
its
k
th box space
.

Example 2.1
.

As a first example, we define the
Temperley-Lieb planar algebra
,
\mathcal{TL}
. Choose the vector spaces to be the Temperley-Lieb algebras
\{\mathcal{TL}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
over
\mathbb{C}
, with a chosen value
\delta>0
. The Temperley-Lieb algebras
[
28
]
were defined diagrammatically by Kauffman
[
16
]
, which we describe here. Elements of
\mathcal{TL}_{k}
are linear combinations of diagrams with
k
points on the top and bottom with nonintersecting strands connecting those
k
points. See figure
4
for examples of elements in
\mathcal{TL}_{3}
. Multiplication,
fg
, of two elements
f
and
g
in
\mathcal{TL}_{k}
is given by stacking
f
on top of
g
. When a “bubble" is formed, it can be removed for a factor of
\delta
(see figure
5
). As an algebra,
\mathcal{TL}_{k}
is generated by elements
X^{\otimes k}
and
\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k-1}
, where
e_{i}
is the diagram with straight strands except a cup and cap in the
(i,i+1)
position.

Now the associated maps to the tangles can be defined through insertion. For example, the map
Z_{M_{k}}:\mathcal{TL}_{k}\to\mathcal{TL}_{k}
associated to the multiplication tangle is the map given by inserting
f\in\mathcal{TL}_{k}
in to the top interior square and
g\in\mathcal{TL}_{k}
into the bottom interior square. The resulting diagram is
fg
. See figure
6
.

f=

g=

Figure 4
:
Two elements of
\mathcal{TL}_{3}
.

=

=\delta

Figure 5
:
Multiplication of
g^{2}
(from figure
4
) in
\mathcal{TL}_{3}
.

=

=

Figure 6
:
The multiplication tangle map in the Temperley-Lieb planar algebra with
f
and
g
of figure
4
plugged in.

The preceding example shows that to best understand the associated multilinear maps to planar tangles, the vector spaces should be described using diagrams. Then the map is just the diagram obtained by inserting diagrams into the interior squares. Therefore, it is best to describe each vector space using diagrammatic generators and skein relations. The
generators
of a planar algebra
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
are a collection of diagrams that can be combined, along with strands, in any arbitrary planar fashion, to obtain any element of its vector spaces. Then any element of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is a linear combination of diagrams that are made up of generators and nonintersecting strands with
k
boundary points on the top and bottom of the diagrams. As an example, if a planar algebra is generated by some diagram
R
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
subject to some skein relations (see figure
7
), then figure
8
gives an element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{3}
, respectively.

Remark
.

The Temperley-Lieb planar algebra is said to have no generators, since every element of every vector space consists of linear combinations of diagrams with only nonintersecting strands.

We define a diagram
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
to be
uncuppable
(respectively,
uncappable
) if for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
e_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, we have that
D_{k}e_{j}=0
(respectively,
e_{j}D_{k}=0
). We define a diagram
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
to be
unsidecappable
if the left and right partial trace of
D_{k}
are both zero. If
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
is uncuppable, uncappable, and unsidecappable, we say
D_{k}
is
U.C.C.S.

\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$R$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 7
:
An example of a potential generator of a planar algebra. This generator would be in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/Rsidecap2.png]
\put(8.0,7.5){$R$}
\end{overpic}

,

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxtensorstrand.png]
\put(4.3,6.2){$R$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 8
:
If a planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
, is generated by
R
in figure
7
, then these would be elements in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{3}
, respectively.

For this paper, our planar algebras will all be unshaded. However, in order to be standard invariants of subfactors, they would also need a checkerboard shading, see
[
3
,
13
,
17
,
18
,
24
]
for examples of planar algebras with checkerboard shading and their connections to subfactors. Beyond a checkerboard shading, subfactor planar algebras
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
need to satisfy the below properties.

1.

\mathcal{P}
is
evaluable
:
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is one-dimensional and for all
k>0
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is finite-dimensional.

2.

\mathcal{P}
is
spherical
: the left and right trace map on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
are equal.

3.

\mathcal{P}
is
positive definite
: there exists an antilinear adjoint map on each vector space, denoted
*:\mathcal{P}_{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}
, that is compatible with reflection over a horizontal line on planar tangles. Further, the sesquilinear form on each
\mathcal{P}_{k}
defined by
\langle f,g\rangle\coloneq\text{tr}(fg^{*})
is positive definite.

An (unshaded) planar algebra that is evaluable, spherical, and postive definite is said to be an (unshaded)
subfactor planar algebra.
We will commonly drop “unshaded" and call both shaded and unshaded cases “subfactor planar algebras".

Example 2.2
.

The Temperley-Lieb planar algebra with chosen
\delta>0
with
\delta^{2}\geq 4
is an unshaded subfactor planar algebra.

Jones
[
14
]
proved that any subfactor of index 4 or greater satisfies that its subfactor planar algebra contains the Temperley-Lieb planar algebra where
\delta^{2}
is the index of the subfactor. We then refer to
\delta^{2}
as the
index
of the planar algebra.

2.2
The Principal Graph

We assume some familiarity with tensor categories. See
[
7
]
, Chapter 2 and 4 for more details on this topic. From a subfactor planar algebra, we can obtain a tensor category. Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
be a subfactor planar algebra. A
projection
in
\mathcal{P}
is an element
P
in the planar algebra such that
P^{2}=P=P^{*}
. A
morphism

f\in\text{Hom}(P,Q)
between two planar algebra elements
P
and
Q
is a diagram whose bottom boundary is
Q
and top boundary is
P
. We say two projections
P
and
Q
are
isomorphic
if and only if there exists a morphism
f\in\text{Hom}(P,Q)
such that
ff^{*}=P
and
f^{*}f=Q
.

Example 2.3
.

The strand
X
is a projection in
\mathcal{TL}
. Further,
\mathcal{TL}_{k}
is a collection of morphisms in
\text{End}(X^{\otimes k})
.

Remark
.

For any subfactor planar algebra,
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}=\text{End}(X^{\otimes k})
.

First create a rigid tensor category from
\mathcal{P}
whose objects are projections and morphisms are morphisms in
\mathcal{P}
. In this category, take
\otimes
to be horizontal concatenation, with unit object
\emptyset
, and dual (which we denote by
\overline{\cdot}
) to be rotation by
-\pi
. Then take the abelian envelope of that category and get the category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{P}}
. That is, objects are direct sums of projections and

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(P_{1}\oplus...\oplus P_{m},Q_{1}\oplus...\oplus Q_{n})=\oplus_{i,j}\text{Hom}(P_{i},Q_{j}).

A projection
P
in
\mathcal{P}
is said to be
minimal
if
\text{End}(P)
is one-dimensional. In the Temperley-Lieb planar algebra, the minimal projections are called the
Jones-Wenzl projections
. For this planar algebra, there is exactly one minimal projection in each box space. We denote
f^{(k)}
to be the minimal projection in
\mathcal{TL}_{k}
. Jones-Wenzl projections are uncuppable, uncappable, and satisfy
Wenzl’s relation

[
29
]
:
f^{(0)}=\emptyset
,
f^{(1)}=X
, and

\displaystyle f^{(k)}\otimes X\cong f^{(k-1)}\otimes f^{(k+1)}.

Since any subfactor planar algebra of index 4 or greater, contains Temperley-Lieb, the planar algebra of this paper (which is of index 4) will contain these projections (but they may no longer be minimal). The trace of Jones-Wenzl projections is well-known. First, we define the
q
-deformation of integers. The
n
th quantum number
is:

\displaystyle[n]_{q}=\frac{q^{n}-a^{-n}}{q-q^{-1}}=q^{n-1}+q^{n-3}+...+q^{-(n-3)}+q^{-(n-1)}.

We can always define
q
such that
\delta=[2]_{q}=q+q^{-1}
. When clear, we drop the subscript
q
. The Jones-Wenzl projections satisfy that
\text{tr}(f^{(k)})=[k+1]_{q}
. In particular,
\text{tr}(f^{(0)})=\text{tr}(\emptyset)=1
,
\text{tr}(f^{(1)})=\text{tr}(X)=\delta
.

For any subfactor planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
, the category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{P}}
is semisimple, i.e., the tensor product of any two objects can be decomposed as a direct sum of minimal projections. How objects decompose can be reflected in a graph called the
principal graph
. We describe a construction of the principal graph. This is not the original construction, but Bisch
[
5
]
showed this yields the same graph. Vertices of a principal graph are isomorphism classes of minimal projections. The number of edges between two vertices
P
and
Q
is the number of times
Q
appears in the direct sum decomposition:

P\otimes X\cong\oplus_{Q\in N(P)}Q,

(1)

where
N(P)
is the set of neighbors of
P
in the principal graph.

Example 2.4
.

From Wenzl’s relation, we can see that the principal graph of the Temperley-Lieb planar algebra is the
A_{\infty}
Dynkin diagram.

Remark
.

In the language of category theory, minimal projections are the simple objects of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{P}}
and the principal graph is its fusion graph.

By taking the trace of both sides of
1
, one finds that the principal graph of a subfactor planar algebra encodes its index. That is,

\displaystyle\delta\text{tr}(P)=\sum_{Q\in N(P)}\text{tr}(Q),

which is called
the trace formula
. Additionally, we have the following useful fact.

Proposition 2.1
.

(Jones
[
14
]
, Theorem 3.1.3) Let
\mathcal{P}
be a subfactor planar algebra. Then
X^{\otimes k}\cong\oplus_{Q\in T_{k}(\emptyset)}Q
, where
T_{k}(\emptyset)
is the multiset of endpoints length
k
from
\emptyset
, including multiples of the same element.

This paper will solely be focused on an index 4 unshaded subfactor planar algebra. Index 4 (shaded) subfactor planar algebras have been classified by Popa
[
25
]
. Popa found that principal graphs of index 4 (shaded) subfactor planar algebras are the
A_{\infty}
Dynkin diagram (when the planar algebra is Temperley-Lieb) and the bipartite affine
ADE
Dynkin diagrams. He also shows that when the principal graph is affine
E_{7}
(which we sometimes will denote
\tilde{E}_{7}
), there is exactly 1 (shaded) subfactor planar algebra, up to planar algebra isomorphism.

2.3
The Jellyfish Algorithm

When defining a planar algebra by generators and relations, showing that
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is one-dimensional is commonly the toughest criteria to check. For the planar algebra in this paper, we will show it is at most one-dimensional by showing there is an evaluation algorithm. In particular, we will describe a
jellyfish algorithm
, as originally defined in
[
3
]
.

Previously, in
[
3
]
, the jellyfish algorithm was applied to a planar algebra with a single generator, say
S\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
. (Note that this paper was handling checkerboard shaded subfactor planar algebras, so had
S
in both shadings,
S_{\pm}\in\mathcal{P}_{k,\pm}
). We outline the process in this case. We commonly refer to a generator such as in figure
7
as a “box". Let
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
. The idea of the jellyfish algorithm is to recognize
D
as being a diagram in a square “tank". When you can pass a box past a strand as well as click strands around boxes, you can float the boxes to the top of the tank with their strands hanging down (resembeling a jellyfish). When clicking the strands around a box, you may need to multiply by a scalar. When moving a box past a strand, you may get a sum of diagrams with more boxes that are moved past the strand and below the boxes is some picture in Temperley-Lieb (we are soon to define “trains" and then can restate this to mean any box with a strand over it can be rewritten as a linear combination of trains). We allow drawing strands all on the bottom of the box or if the box is attached to its neighbor, on the right or left sides. It is implied that these strands rainbow over the box on the right. We call a diagram at this point in
jellyfish form
. Below, in figure
9
, is an example where the diagram on the left is floated to the top with the right-hand side picture being one of its resulting summands in jellyfish form.

\begin{overpic}[fig/jellyfishex1.png]
\put(22.5,24.0){$S$}
\put(17.0,35.0){$S$}
\put(36.5,27.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 9
:
A schematic illustration of the first steps of the jellyfish algorithm.

Now, since strands cannot cross, a graph theoretical argument given in
[
3
]
(Theorem 3.8) gives that either an
S
is cupped or there exists at least one pair of adjacent boxes that are connected by at least
k
strands. That is, the two
S
-boxes form an
S^{2}
. If there is no cup, and it is true that
S^{2}
is a linear combination of diagrams with at least one less
S
, then eventually we can reduce the diagram to a linear combination of diagrams with zero or one
S
-box. In the summands where there are no
S
-boxes, the diagram is closed and in Temperley-Lieb, so can be evaluated to a number by popping bubbles. In the summands where there is exactly one
S
-box,
S
must be cupped, capped, or side-capped somewhere since the diagram is closed. If
S
is known to be U.C.C.S, then these summands are zero. This would then give that
D
can be evaluated.

Below, in Proposition
2.2
, we will list the relations that would be sufficient to have a jellyfish algorithm on a single generator. The first two relations allow the boxes to be floated to the top of the tank, and the latter two relations allow the diagram in jellyfish form to be evaluated. Before stating this propostion, however, we give a few definitions.

Recall that in a rigid monoidal category, every object
a
, has a dual
\overline{a}
, with
evaluation
and
coevaluation
maps
\text{ev}_{a}:\overline{a}\otimes a\to\emptyset
,
\text{coev}_{a}:\emptyset\to a\otimes\overline{a}
satisfying "zig-zag" identities
[
7
]
(Definition 2.10.1). Diagrammatically,
\text{ev}_{X}
is a cap and
\text{coev}_{X}
is a cup.

In this paper, we need some common diagrams. Notice that for
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, we can write the following diagram in figure
10(a)
as
\text{ev}_{X}(X\otimes\text{ev}_{X}\otimes X)(X\otimes X\otimes\text{ev}_{X}\otimes X\otimes X)...(D_{k}\otimes X^{\otimes k})
. We will define
\mathcal{R}_{k}(D_{k})\coloneq\text{ev}_{X}(X\otimes\text{ev}_{X}\otimes X)(X\otimes X\otimes\text{ev}_{X}\otimes X\otimes X)...(D_{k}\otimes X^{\otimes k})
. We can think of
\mathcal{R}
as “rainbowing" the strands on top of
D_{k}
to the right. We will drop the subscript when the box space is clear. Another common diagram is a big cap over the rainbowed-box. That is, if
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
,
e_{1}(X\otimes\mathcal{R}(D_{k})\otimes X)
. This looks like figure
10(b)
and is denoted as
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(D_{k}))
. Notice that for
D_{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
that
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(D_{k}))
diagrammatically looks like figure
10(c)
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/g2.png]
\put(5.0,9.0){$D_{k}$}
\put(5.0,3.0){$...$}
\put(15.4,3.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}

(a)

The “rainbowed" diagram
\mathcal{R}(D_{k})
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/capoverbox.png]
\put(5.0,9.0){$D_{k}$}
\put(6.0,3.0){$...$}
\put(16.0,3.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}

(b)

The “over the rainbow", diagram
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(D_{k}))
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/clickrainbow.png]
\put(7.5,9.0){$D_{k}$}
\put(8.0,3.0){$...$}
\put(18.0,3.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}

(c)

The diagram
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(D_{k}))
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a planar algebra and
S\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
be a box. A
train
(in
S
)
[
2
]
is a diagram where the top boundary is a finite collection of rainbowed
S
-boxes’s (in particular, there are no strands capped over the
S
-boxes) on top of a diagram in Temperley-Lieb. That is, diagrammatically a train in
S
looks like figure
11
. We say
S
satisfies a
jellyfish relation
if
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))
can be written as a linear combination of trains. Diagrammatically the jellyfish relation is shown in figure
12
. While the definition of train first appeared in
[
2
]
, it’s equivalent notion in terms of
\lambda
-lattices first appeared in
[
26
]
. If a
k
-box
S
in a planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
has a jellyfish relation, we say
S
is a
jellyfish generator (at depth
k
)
.

Proposition 2.2
.

(Bigelow–Morrison–Peters–Snyder
[
3
]
, Theorem 3.8) A planar algebra generated by a single box
S\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
satisfying the below criteria

(i)

There exists an
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathcal{F}(S)=aS
,

(ii)

S
has a jellyfish relation,

(iii)

there exists
b,c\in\mathbb{C}
such that
S^{2}=bS+cf^{(k)}
, and

(iv)

S
is U.C.C.S.,

can be evaluated using the jellyfish algorithm.

\end{overpic}

D\in\mathcal{TL}
(and implicitly the strands of

are rainbowed over, but drawn for convenience below the box).

\end{overpic}

={{{\sum_{i}}}}

3. Results on General Index 4 Planar Algebras

We first state and prove some results true for any index 4 planar algebras.

Remark
.

Wenzl’s relation can be written as

\displaystyle f^{(k+1)}=f^{(k)}\otimes X-\frac{[k]_{q}}{[k+1]_{q}}(f^{(k)}\otimes X)e_{k}(f^{(k)}\otimes X).

Lemma 3.1
.

For any index 4 planar algebra,
[k]_{q}=k
,
f^{(2)}=X\otimes X-\frac{1}{2}e_{1}
,
f^{(3)}=X^{\otimes 3}-\frac{2}{3}e_{1}-\frac{2}{3}e_{2}+\frac{1}{3}e_{2}e_{1}+\frac{1}{3}e_{1}e_{2}
, and
\text{tr}(f^{(k)})=k+1
for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\text{tr}(f^{(k)})=[k+1]_{q}
and
[k]_{q}=q^{k-1}+q^{k-3}+...+q^{-(k-3)}+q^{-(k-1)}
. We first prove by induction on
k\geq 0
that
\text{tr}(f^{(k)})(=[k+1]_{q})=k+1
. We know that
\text{tr}(f^{(0)})=[1]_{q}=1
and
\delta=[2]_{q}=q+q^{-1}=2
, which gives that
q=1
. Suppose the claim is true for
k\geq 1
. Then
[k+1]_{q}=q^{(k+1)-1}+[k-1]_{q}+q^{-((k+1)-1)}=1+(k-1)+1=k+1
.

By Wenzl’s relation,
f^{(2)}=f^{(1)}\otimes X\cong\frac{1}{2}(f^{(1)}\otimes X)e_{1}(f^{(1)}\otimes X)
. Since
f^{(1)}=X
, we get
f^{(2)}=X\otimes X-\frac{1}{2}e_{1}
.

We employ the same method for
f^{(3)}
. Wenzl’s relation gives that
f^{(3)}=f^{(2)}\otimes X-\frac{2}{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)
. Expanding out
f^{(2)}
gives

\displaystyle f^{(3)}

\displaystyle=(X\otimes X-\frac{1}{2}e_{1})\otimes X-\frac{2}{3}((X\otimes X-\frac{1}{2}e_{1})\otimes X)e_{2}((X\otimes X-\frac{1}{2}e_{1})\otimes X)

\displaystyle=X^{\otimes 3}-\frac{2}{3}e_{1}-\frac{2}{3}e_{2}+\frac{1}{3}e_{2}e_{1}+\frac{1}{3}e_{1}e_{2}

as we wished.
∎

We define a
leaf
of a graph to be a vertex with only 1 neighbor. When the context is clear, we may also refer to a
leaf
as the minimal projection associated to a leaf vertex. We call the following equality the
leaf property
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
be a leaf of a principal graph for any planar algebra of index 4. Then
L\otimes X=2(L\otimes X)e_{k}(L\otimes X)
.

Proof.

Let
P
be the only neighbor of
L
on the principal graph. Then
L\otimes X\cong P
. This gives
\text{Hom}(L\otimes X,L\otimes X)\cong\text{Hom}(P,P)
is 1-dimensional since
P
is a minimal projection. Since
(L\otimes X)e_{k}(L\otimes X)
is in
\text{Hom}(L\otimes X,L\otimes X)
, this gives there exists an
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that
(L\otimes X)e_{k}(L\otimes X)=a(L\otimes X)^{2}=a(L\otimes X)
. Taking the trace of both sides gives
a=\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

We will frequently use that for all
k\geq 1
, the coefficient of the
X^{\otimes k}
diagram in the expansion of
f^{(k)}
is always 1. The rest of the terms involve cups and caps. See
[
22
]
, Lemma 2.1 for a proof of this fact.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an arbitrary planar algebra. If
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
is uncuppable and uncappable, then
D_{k}(f^{(j)}\otimes X^{\otimes(k-j)})=(f^{(j)}\otimes X^{\otimes(k-j)})D_{k}=D_{k}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
. If further
D_{k}
is U.C.C.S., then
\mathcal{R}(D_{k})(f^{(j)}\otimes X^{\otimes(2k-j)})=(f^{(j)}\otimes X^{\otimes(2k-j)})\mathcal{R}(D_{k})=\mathcal{R}(D_{k})
for all
1\leq j\leq 2k
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an arbitrary planar algebra. Let
D_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
be uncuppable and uncappable. All terms except the identity of
f^{(j)}
has a cup and cap. The identity has coefficient 1. Thus
D_{k}(f^{(j)}\otimes X^{\otimes(k-j)})=D_{k}(X^{\otimes j}\otimes X^{(k-j)})=D_{k}
. The same idea proves the rest of the equalities.
∎

The above properties proved for uncuppable, uncappable, and U.C.C.S. diagrams are called
absorption rules
. We say
D_{k}
(respectively,
\mathcal{R}(D_{k})
)
absorbs

f^{(j)}
(respectively,
f^{(2k-j)}
).

Lemma 3.4
.

If
Q
is a projection in the
k
th box space of an arbitrary planar algebra and
P
is defined by
P\coloneq Q\otimes X-a(Q\otimes X)e_{k}(Q\otimes X)
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
, then we have the following absorption rule:
P(Q\otimes X)=(Q\otimes X)P=P
.

Proof.

By the definition of
P
,
P(Q\otimes X)=(Q\otimes X)^{2}-a(Q\otimes X)e_{k}(Q\otimes X)^{2}=Q\otimes X-a(Q\otimes X)e_{k}(Q\otimes X)=P
. The case
(Q\otimes X)P
is proved the same way.
∎

We call the rule proved in Lemma
3.4
, the
recursive absorption rule
.

Define
g_{n,i}
to be the diagram in Temperley-Lieb with
n
strands on the top and bottom, a cap in the
(n-1,n)
position and a cup in the
(i,i+1)
position. Define
g_{n,n}
to be the identity on
n
strands. Below are
g_{4,4},g_{4,3},g_{4,2},
and
g_{4,1}
respectively.

\begin{overpic}[fig/gelements.png]
\put(8.0,0.0){,}
\put(21.0,0.0){,}
\put(33.0,0.0){,}
\end{overpic}

We will occasionally employ the following theorem due to Frenkel and Khovanov
[
9
]
, adapted for our index 4 case. Note that the statement below is somewhat different from that in their paper because of different conventions. (Also see Morrison
[
22
]
, Proposition 3.3, for another formulation of this statement.)

Proposition 3.1
.

(Frenkel–Khovanov
[
9
]
, Theorem 3.5) Let
\mathcal{P}
be an arbitrary planar algebra of index 4. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the Jones-Wenzl projections satisfy

\displaystyle f^{(n+1)}=\frac{n+1}{n+1}g_{n+1,n+1}(f^{(n)}\otimes X)-\frac{n}{n+1}g_{n+1,n}(f^{(n)}\otimes X)+...\pm\frac{1}{n+1}g_{n+1,1}(f^{(n)}\otimes X).

4. Sufficient Relations for Affine
E_{7}
Unshaded Subfactor Planar Algebras

We begin by giving the sufficient relations for the affine
E_{7}
(unshaded) subfactor planar algebra. In the next section, we show that these relations are necessary. All boxes have a marked point on its left middle boundary. If drawn, it is denoted with a star.

Definition 4.1
.

Define the following:

\begin{overpic}[fig/crossing.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{14.22636pt}{{$\coloneq i$}}}}\quad\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/asmoothing.png]
\end{overpic}{\raisebox{14.22636pt}{{$-i$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt\begin{overpic}[fig/bsmoothing.png]
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt.

(2)

As a consequence, this also defines,

\begin{overpic}[fig/othercrossing.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{14.22636pt}{{$\coloneq i$}}}}\quad\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/bsmoothing.png]
\end{overpic}{\raisebox{14.22636pt}{{$-i$}}}\hskip 2.84526pt\begin{overpic}[fig/asmoothing.png]
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt.

(3)

We will denote the crossing in equation
2
by
\mathfrak{X}
. The diagram in the expansion of
\mathfrak{X}
with the coefficient
i
is called an
A-smoothing
, denoted by
\mathfrak{A}
, and the diagram with the coefficient
-i
is called a
B-smoothing
, denoted by
\mathfrak{B}
. The crossing in equation
3
is denoted by
\mathfrak{Y}
.

We will focus this section on proving the below theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the planar algebra generated by a self-adjoint box
S\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
:

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$S$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic},

with the * extended anti-linearly and on diagrams, modulo the following relations:

(i)

(
the bubble relation
)
\hbox to20.72pt{\vbox to20.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.35841pt\lower-10.35841pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 13.78 0 C 13.78 7.61 7.61 13.78 0 13.78 C -7.61 13.78 -13.78 7.61 -13.78 0 C -13.78 -7.61 -7.61 -13.78 0 -13.78 C 7.61 -13.78 13.78 -7.61 13.78 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=2
,

(ii)

(
S
is U.C.C.S.
)

\begin{aligned} \begin{overpic}[fig/Scap1.png]
\put(2.5,7.0){$S$}
\put(2.5,2.5){$...$}
\put(4.8,15.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=...=$}}}}\quad\quad\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/Scap2.png]
\put(2.5,7.5){$S$}
\put(2.5,2.5){$...$}
\put(1.0,15.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=$}}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/Scup.png]
\put(3.0,7.5){$S$}
\put(5.1,2.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(2.5,14.5){$...$}
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=...=$}}}}\quad\quad\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/Ssidecap.png]
\put(2.0,7.5){$S$}
\put(2.1,2.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(2.5,14.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=$}}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/Ssidecap2.png]
\put(8.0,7.5){$S$}
\put(9.1,2.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(9.1,14.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=0$,}}}}\end{aligned}

(iii)

(
S^{2}
relation
)

\begin{aligned} \begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(3.6,6.2){$S^{2}$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=6$}}}\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(3.2,6.2){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$+$}}}\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(3.6,6.3){$S$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}\end{aligned}

(iv)

(
Jellyfish relation
)

\begin{aligned} \begin{overpic}[fig/capoverbox.png]
\put(4.5,6.4){$S$}
\put(12.3,2.0){$...$}
\put(4.5,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{17.07182pt}{{$=$}}}\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithbelowcrossings.png]
\put(7.7,12.5){$S$}
\put(16.1,2.0){$...$}
\put(8.3,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}\end{aligned}

Then
\mathcal{P}
is an unshaded subfactor planar algebra with the below principal graph:

[\emptyset]
[P_{1}]
[P_{2}]
[Q_{4}]
[P_{3}]
[P_{4}]
[P_{5}]
[P_{6}]

(4)

where
P_{1}\cong f^{(1)}=X
,
P_{2}\cong f^{(2)}
,
P_{3}\cong f^{(3)}
,
P_{4}\cong\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S
,
P_{5}\cong P_{4}\otimes X-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)
,
P_{6}\cong P_{5}\otimes X-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)
, and
Q_{4}\cong\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S
.

Define the planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
and elements
P_{1},...,P_{6},
and
Q_{4}
as in the preceding theorem. We first show that these elements are projections.

Lemma 4.2
.

Q_{4}
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
,
P_{3}
, and
P_{4}
are projections. Further, they are all uncuppable and uncappable.

Proof.

Q_{4}
is a linear combination of two self-adjoint, uncuppable, and uncappable elements so is itself self-adjoint, uncuppable, and uncappable. Further, using that
S
is U.C.C.S. and the
S^{2}
relation,

\displaystyle Q_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{25}(4f^{(4)}+4S+S^{2})=\frac{1}{25}(4f^{(4)}+4S+6f^{(4)}+S)=\frac{1}{25}(10f^{(4)}+5S)=\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S=Q_{4}

so
Q_{4}
is a projection.

Since
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
, and
P_{3}
are Jones-Wenzl projections, all that is left to show is that
P_{4}
is a uncuppable and uncappable projection.
P_{4}
is a linear combination of two self-adjoint, uncuppable, and uncappable elements so is itself self-adjoint, uncuppable, and uncappable. Further, using that
S
is U.C.C.S. and the
S^{2}
relation, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{25}(9f^{(4)}-6S+S^{2})=\frac{1}{25}(9f^{(4)}-6S+6f^{(4)}+S)=\frac{1}{25}(15f^{(4)}-5S)=\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S=P_{4}

so
P_{4}
is also a projection.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

We have the following partial traces:

(i)

E_{2}(f^{(2)})=\frac{3}{2}f^{(1)}=\frac{3}{2}X
,

(ii)

E_{3}(f^{(3)})=\frac{4}{3}f^{(2)}
,

(iii)

E_{4}(f^{(4)})=\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}
,

(iv)

E_{5}(P_{5})=\frac{2}{3}P_{4}
,

(v)

E_{4}(Q_{4})=\frac{1}{2}f^{(3)}
, and

(vi)

E_{4}(P_{4})=\frac{3}{4}f^{(3)}
.

Proof.

By Wenzl’s relation,

E_{2}(f^{(2)})=2f^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2}{f^{(1)}}^{2}=\frac{3}{2}f^{(1)}
,
E_{3}(f^{(3)})=2f^{(2)}-\frac{2}{3}{f^{(2)}}^{2}=\frac{4}{3}f^{(2)}
, and
E_{4}(f^{(4)})=2f^{(3)}-\frac{3}{4}{f^{(3)}}^{2}=\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}
. Using that
P_{4}
is a projection,
E_{5}(P_{5})=2P_{4}-\frac{4}{3}P_{4}^{2}=2P_{4}-\frac{4}{3}P_{4}=\frac{2}{3}P_{4}
. Using that
S
is U.C.C.S.,
E_{4}(Q_{4})=\frac{2}{5}E_{4}(f^{(4)})+\frac{1}{5}E_{4}(S)=\frac{2}{5}\cdot\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}=\frac{1}{2}f^{(3)}
. Similarly,
E_{4}(P_{4})=\frac{3}{5}E_{4}(f^{(4)})-\frac{1}{5}E_{4}(S)=\frac{3}{5}\cdot\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}=\frac{3}{4}f^{(3)}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

P_{5}
is a projection.

Proof.

Since
P_{4}
is self-adjoint, so is
P_{5}
. Further,

\displaystyle P_{5}^{2}

\displaystyle=(P_{4}\otimes X)^{2}-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)^{2}e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)^{2}+\frac{16}{9}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})E_{4}(P_{4})(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes 4)

\displaystyle=(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)+\frac{16}{9}\cdot\frac{3}{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})f^{(3)}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes 4)

\displaystyle=(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{8}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)+\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})f^{(3)}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes 4)

P_{4}
is uncuppable and uncappable, so the last term in the above sum becomes
\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)
.
This gives
P_{5}^{2}=P_{4}\otimes X-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)=P_{5}
, as we wished.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

The partial trace of
P_{6}
is
\frac{1}{2}P_{5}
. That is,
E_{6}(P_{6})=\frac{1}{2}P_{5}
. Also
P_{6}
is a projection.

Proof.

Since
P_{5}^{2}=P_{5}
, we have that
E_{6}(P_{6})=2P_{5}-\frac{3}{2}P_{5}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}P_{5}
. We can calculate the square of
P_{6}
using the partial trace of
P_{5}
and that
P_{5}
is a projection:

\displaystyle P_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle=(P_{5}\otimes X)^{2}-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)^{2}e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)^{2}+\frac{9}{4}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})E_{5}(P_{5})(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)

\displaystyle=(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{6}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)+\frac{9}{4}\cdot\frac{2}{3}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{4}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)

The recursive absorption rule of Lemma
3.4
gives that

\displaystyle P_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle=(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{6}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)+\frac{9}{4}\cdot\frac{2}{3}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)

\displaystyle=(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{6}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)+\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)

\displaystyle=P_{5}\otimes X-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)=P_{6}.

So indeed
P_{6}
is a projection.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

The traces of the projections are as follows.

(i)

\text{tr}(\emptyset)=1
,

(ii)

\text{tr}(P_{1})=2
,

(iii)

\text{tr}(P_{2})=3
,

(iv)

\text{tr}(P_{3})=4
,

(v)

\text{tr}(P_{4})=3
,

(vi)

\text{tr}(P_{5})=2
,

(vii)

\text{tr}(P_{6})=1
, and

(viii)

\text{tr}(Q_{4})=2
.

Proof.

By convention
\text{tr}(\emptyset)=1
. By the bubble relation,
\text{tr}(P_{1})=2
. We use the partial traces found in Lemma
4.3
and
4.5
to compute

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{2})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{2}(P_{2}))=\frac{3}{2}\text{tr}(P_{1})=3,

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{3})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{3}(P_{3}))=\frac{4}{3}\text{tr}(P_{2})=4,

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{4})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{4}(P_{4}))=\frac{3}{4}\text{tr}(P_{3})=3,

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{5})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{5}(P_{5}))=\frac{2}{3}\text{tr}(P_{4})=2,

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{6})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{6}(P_{6}))=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}(P_{5})=1,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\text{tr}(Q_{4})

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(E_{4}(Q_{4}))=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}(P_{3})=2,

as we wished.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

We have the following relationships between projections:

(i)

P_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X)=(P_{4}\otimes X)P_{5}=P_{5}
,

(ii)

P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X)=(P_{5}\otimes X)P_{6}=P_{6}
,

(iii)

P_{4}f^{(3)}=f^{(3)}P_{4}=P_{4}
, and

(iv)

P_{6}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})P_{6}=P_{6}
.

Proof.

The first two parts follow from the recursive absorption rule. The second part follows from
P_{4}
being uncuppable and uncappable. The fourth part is clear using the previous parts as

\displaystyle P_{6}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X)(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X)=P_{6}

A symmetric argument also shows
(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})P_{6}=P_{6}
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

P_{5}
and
P_{6}
are uncuppable and uncappable.

Proof.

For
P_{5},
if
1\leq k\leq 3
, then
e_{k}P_{5}=P_{5}e_{k}=0
, since
P_{4}
is uncuppable and uncappable. What’s left to show is that
e_{4}P_{5}=P_{5}e_{4}=0
. By symmetry, we will only compute
e_{4}P_{5}
. Notice,

\displaystyle e_{4}P_{5}

\displaystyle=e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})E_{4}(P_{4})(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes X)=e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}\cdot\frac{3}{4}e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)=0.

For
P_{6}
, we use that
P_{5}
is uncuppable and uncappable to obtain that
e_{k}P_{6}=P_{6}e_{k}=0
for
1\leq k\leq 4
. By symmetry, all that is left to show is
e_{5}P_{6}=0
. Using the partial trace of
P_{5}:

\displaystyle e_{5}P_{6}

\displaystyle=e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{3}{2}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})E_{5}(P_{5})(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)

\displaystyle=e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)-\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{3}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{4}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)

Using the absorption rule of Lemma
3.4
, we obtain
e_{5}P_{6}=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

In
\mathcal{P}
, we have the following tensor products:

(i)

\emptyset\otimes X\cong P_{1}
,

(ii)

P_{1}\otimes X\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}
,

(iii)

P_{2}\otimes X\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}
,

(iv)

P_{3}\otimes X\cong P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}
,

(v)

P_{4}\otimes X\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
, and

(vi)

P_{5}\otimes X\cong P_{4}\oplus P_{6}
.

Proof.

Part (i), (ii), and (iii) follow from Wenzl’s relation. For part (iv), we can notice that
P_{4}+Q_{4}=f^{(4)}
, so this also follows from Wenzl’s relation.

For part (v), we claim the explicit isomorphisms are:

\displaystyle A_{1}\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{3}&(P_{4}\otimes X)P_{5}\end{bmatrix}:P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\to P_{4}\otimes X,\text{ and }

\displaystyle B_{1}\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{4}{3}P_{3}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes X)\\
P_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X)\end{bmatrix}:P_{4}\otimes X\to P_{3}\oplus P_{5}

Notice, we can more conveniently recognize

\displaystyle A_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})&P_{5}\end{bmatrix}\text{ and }B_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{4}{3}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes X)\\
P_{5}\end{bmatrix}

Then, we can see that
A_{1}B_{1}=\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)+P_{5}=P_{4}\otimes X
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle B_{1}A_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{4}{3}E_{4}(P_{4})&\frac{4}{3}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes X)P_{5}\\
P_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})&P_{5}\end{bmatrix}

By the recursive absorption rule, the off-diagonal entries are 0. Since
E_{4}(P_{4})=\frac{3}{4}f^{(3)}
, we obtain
B_{1}A_{1}=P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
.

For part (vi) we claim the explicit isomorphisms are:

\displaystyle A_{2}\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{4}&(P_{5}\otimes X)P_{6}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})&P_{6}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle B_{2}\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{3}{2}P_{4}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)\\
P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{3}{2}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)\\
P_{6}\end{bmatrix}.

So
A_{2}\in\text{Hom}(P_{4}\oplus P_{6},P_{5}\otimes X)
and
B_{2}\in\text{Hom}(P_{5}\otimes X,P_{4}\oplus P_{6})
. We also have that

\displaystyle A_{2}B_{2}=\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)+P_{6}=P_{5}\otimes X

and

\displaystyle B_{2}A_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{3}{2}E_{5}(P_{5})&\frac{3}{2}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)P_{6}\\
P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})&P_{6}\end{bmatrix}

By the recursive absorption rule, the off-diagonals are zero. Since
E_{5}(P_{5})=\frac{2}{3}P_{4}
, we obtain
B_{2}A_{2}=P_{4}\oplus P_{6}
. Thus,
P_{4}\oplus P_{6}\cong P_{5}\otimes X
.
∎

4.1
Results Involving Crossings in an Index 4 Planar Algebra

Next, we give a collection of proofs for general planar algebras with a defined crossing. Then we conclude this subsection with some results about
\mathcal{P}
and show that we have the leaf relations for
\tilde{E}_{7}
. Recall a classical theorem of Reidemeister
[
27
]
that says two knots (or links) are equivalent if any only if they differ by a finite sequence of the below
Reidemeister moves
and planar isotopies:

\begin{aligned} &{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{{Reidemeister I:}}}}\hskip 8.53581pt\begin{overpic}[fig/strand.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\leftrightarrow$}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/reid1.png]
\end{overpic}\\
&{\raisebox{5.69046pt}{{{Reidemeister II:}}}}\hskip 8.53581pt\begin{overpic}[fig/XtensorX.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\leftrightarrow$}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/reid2.png]
\end{overpic}\end{aligned}

Reidemeister III:

\begin{overpic}[fig/reid3a.png]
\end{overpic}

\leftrightarrow

\begin{overpic}[fig/reid3b.png]
\end{overpic}

Lemma 4.10
.

Definition
4.1
agrees with the bubble relation and Reidemeister II and III.

Proof.

Since we have for
q=1
,
\text{tr}(X)=q+q^{-1}
, and
\mathfrak{X}=\sqrt{-q}\mathfrak{A}+\sqrt{-q}^{-1}\mathfrak{B}
, we exactly have the Kauffman bracket, first defined by Kauffman in
[
16
]
. This is well known in knot theory to satisfy Reidemeister II and III.
∎

We do not have Reidemeister I. Instead, we have the following.

Lemma 4.11
.

If an arbitrary planar algebra has crossings defined as in Definition
4.1
, then we have the following rules for the crossings:

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/crossingcup.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{{$=-i$}}}\hskip 5.69054pt{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/cup.png]
\end{overpic}}}\quad{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{{\text{ and }}}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/othercrossingcup.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{{$=i$}}}\hskip 5.69054pt{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/cup.png]
\end{overpic}}}.

Proof.

A direct application of Definition
4.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
R
be an uncuppable box in the
n
th box space of an arbitrary planar algebra with crossings defined as in Definition
4.1
. Then

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbotto.png]
\put(7.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(6.8,-2.0){\tiny{$(m)$}}
\put(1.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(4.0,17.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

=(-i)^{m}R

and

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithundercrossingonbotto.png]
\put(8.6,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(7.2,-2.0){\tiny{$(m)$}}
\put(2.4,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.3,17.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

=i^{m}R

where
0\leq m\leq n
indicates the number of strands crossed over or under.

Proof.

We will prove both cases by induction on
m
. The case when
m=0
is clear. Assume the lemma is true for
0\leq m<n
. Then for
m+1
, resolve the left most crossing. When strand is overcrossing
m+1
strands, the crossing is of the form
\mathfrak{Y}
. Recall
\mathfrak{Y}=i\mathfrak{B}-i\mathfrak{A}
. The
B
-smoothing will cup
R
, and thus that term will be zero. The
A
-smoothing will result in the strand overcrossing
m
strands, which by induction is
(-i)^{m}R
. Multiplying by the
-i
coefficient will then result in
(-i)^{m+1}R
. The undercrossing case is nearly the same. In this case, we have a crossing of the form
\mathfrak{X}
. The
A
-smoothing has a coefficient of
i
and the
B
-smoothing with cap
R
.
∎

The same idea is used for the next proof, so will be omitted.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
R
be an uncuppable box in the
n
th box space of an arbitrary planar algebra with crossing defined as in Definition
4.1
. Then

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottomleft.png]
\put(9.1,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(3.2,-2.0){\tiny{$(m)$}}
\put(3.8,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

=i^{m}R

and

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithundercrossingonbottomleft.png]
\put(9.6,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(3.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(m)$}}
\put(3.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.3,17.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

=(-i)^{m}R

where
0\leq m\leq n
indicates the number of strands crossed over or under.

Lemma 4.14
.

When resolving all the crossings of the below diagram,

\begin{overpic}[fig/overcrossing2strands.png]
\put(4.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(5)

where
c\geq 1
is the number of strands crossed over, the coefficient of the term

\begin{overpic}[fig/captwodiagcup.png]
\put(8.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(8.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(6)

is
(-i)^{c}
. Similarly, when resolving all the crossings of

\begin{overpic}[fig/overcrossing2strandsotherway.png]
\put(4.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(7)

where
c\geq 1
is the number of strands crossed over, the coefficient of the term

\begin{overpic}[fig/cuptwodiagcap.png]
\put(4.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(2.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(8)

is
i^{c}
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
c
. When
c=1
, there is a single crossing whose resolution is given by Definition
4.1
. Suppose the result is true for
c\geq 1
. By resolving the left-most crossing we obtain

\begin{overpic}[fig/overcrossing2strands.png]
\put(4.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(2.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(c+1)$}}
\end{overpic}

=i

\begin{overpic}[fig/strandovercrossing2strands.png]
\put(7.6,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(7.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

-i

\begin{overpic}[fig/cupcapovercrossing.png]
\put(5.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.4,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(9)

The first term on the right-hand side will not have a term of the form (
6
) since the left-most strand is straight with no crossings. Thus, we focus our attention to the second term on the right-hand side of the above equality. Notice that the term that will result in the form (
6
) for
c+1
will be the term where diagram in (
5
) resolves to the diagram in (
6
) for
c
. By induction, the coefficient of this term is
(-i)^{c}
. Thus, due to the
-i
in front of the term in equation
9
, the coefficient will be
(-i)^{c+1}
for the desired term. The other case is nearly identical in proof.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Switching an even number of crossings in any diagram of the planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
results in the same diagram.

Proof.

Suppose there are two local neighborhoods,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
, that just consist of a crossing. Denote the crossings or smoothings in these neighborhoods by multiplication. That is,
\mathfrak{X}\mathfrak{Y}
indicates that in
N_{1}
is an
\mathfrak{X}
crossing and in
N_{2}
is a
\mathfrak{Y}
crossing. It is an easy computation to see that
\mathfrak{X}\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{Y}\mathfrak{Y}=-\mathfrak{B}\mathfrak{B}+\mathfrak{B}\mathfrak{A}+\mathfrak{A}\mathfrak{B}-\mathfrak{A}\mathfrak{A}=-\mathfrak{X}\mathfrak{Y}=-\mathfrak{Y}\mathfrak{X}
.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

We introduce the following notation for convenience. Let
R
be a box in the
n
th box space of a planar algebra.

1.

We will denote the following for
0\leq m\leq n
:

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$R$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\put(1.9,-2.0){\tiny{$(2n-m)$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\coloneq$}}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/partialrainbowedbox.png]
\put(4.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(11.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(n-m)$}}
\put(13.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(4.0,6.2){$R$}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{\text{and}}}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$R$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(n-m)$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\coloneq$}}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/partialrainbowedbox2.png]
\put(2.0,14.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(0.8,19.0){\tiny{$(m)$}}
\put(10.5,15.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(9.5,8.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}.

2.

We can color
R
by any color, say blue. Then drop the box and instead notate where one side of the strands of
R
touch. It will be clear from context if these will be the top or bottom strands of
R
. That is,

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/bluetwostrands.png]

\put(-0.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(n-m)$}}
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\coloneq$}}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/bluebox.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$R$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(2n-m)$}}
\end{overpic}.

3.

We will sometimes denote
n
black, unoriented parallel strands by

.

We will denote the following:

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
k,\ell\geq 8
. Let
R
and
S
be two U.C.C.S. boxes in the
k
th box space of a planar algebra colored red and blue respectively. Let
D
be an uncuppable and uncappable box colored green in the
\ell
th box space of the planar algebra. Then when resolving the crossings of the below term,

\begin{overpic}[fig/coloredovercrossing.png]
\put(10.0,1.5){$...$}
\put(23.0,1.5){$...$}
\put(8.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(9-c)$}}
\put(24.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(10)

where
1\leq c\leq 7
is the number of red dots on the bottom,
9-c
is the number of green dots, and there are 8 blue dots at the top, the coefficient of the below term

\begin{overpic}[fig/coloredresolvedofovercrossing.png]
\put(22.0,2.0){$...$}
\put(5.0,2.0){$...$}
\put(3.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(9-c)$}}
\put(24.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\end{overpic}

(11)

is
(-1)^{c}
.

Proof.

Fix some
c
, where
1\leq c\leq 7
. Resolving the rightmost crossing of the diagram (
10
), the
A
-smoothing will give 0 since
R
is U.C.C.S. The
B
-smoothing will have a
-i
coefficient in front of it. Then, use Lemma
4.14
for the crossing over the leftmost
8-c
green dots which gives
i^{9-c-1}
times the diagram

\displaystyle\scalebox{1.6}{\begin{overpic}[fig/coloredresolvedofovercrossing2.png]
\put(4.5,1.5){$...$}
\put(3.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(9-c)$}}
\put(21.0,1.5){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}}.

Then again using Lemma
4.14
over the
c-1
red dots on the bottom will give the desired term times a coefficient of
(-i)^{c-1}
. Thus the coefficient of the term will be
-i(i)^{8-c}(-i)^{c-1}=(-i)^{c}i^{-c}=(-1)^{c}
, as desired.
∎

Now we return to proofs regarding the planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
defined in Theorem
4.1
.

Lemma 4.17
.

In the planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{F}(S)=S
.

Proof.

This is equvalent to showing
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))=\mathcal{R}(S)
. By using the jellyfish relation,
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))
is equivalent to the diagram

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottomtwist.png]
\put(7.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(7)$}}
\put(7.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84544pt.

By Lemma
4.11
, the leftmost crossing can be resolved to become

-i
\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottom2.png]
\put(7.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(7)$}}
\put(7.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}

Since
S
is U.C.C.S., Lemma
4.12
gives that the above is equal to

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottom4.png]
\put(15.5,-2.0){\tiny{$(4)$}}
\put(6.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(15.5,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84544pt.

Resolving the leftmost crossing is a linear combination of two terms, one of which is zero since it is sidecapping the
S
. The other term is

\displaystyle\raisebox{28.45274pt}{$-i$}\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottom5.png]
\put(16.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(3)$}}
\put(6.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(16.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}.

Using Lemma
4.12
again gives
(-i)^{4}\mathcal{R}(S)=\mathcal{R}(S)
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

In
\mathcal{P}
, the below diagram is zero for

and

.

Proof.

and

. We just show the

Lemma 4.21
.

for

f^{(10)}

\begin{overpic}[fig/211.png]

\put(16.0,17.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(22.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}

(17)

Recall that we assumed
T_{i}
was not the identity, so
T_{i}
and thus
R_{i}
, has some cap. Since
f^{(14)}
and
S
are uncappable, there is a cap between strands 8 and 9. In this case, there are two other options of
R_{i}
, the rest of the strands are straight, or there is a cap between strands 7 and 10.

Consider the terms in the left sum of equation
17
. If
R_{i}
has a cap between strands 8 and 9 and otherwise has no caps then we get the term

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelow.png]
\put(28.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(0.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(5.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,24.0){\tiny{$1$}}
\put(23.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(11.0,8.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt,

which by Lemma
4.20
is zero. If
R_{i}
has another cap between strands 7 and 10 then there must be an additional cup on top, which cups
S
and is zero. The first summand is thus zero.

Consider the terms in the second sum of equation
17
. If
R_{i}
has a cap between strands 8 and 9 and otherwise no caps, then we get the term

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelow.png]
\put(28.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(0.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(4.0,25.0){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(15.0,24.0){\tiny{$1$}}
\put(23.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(10.0,8.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt.

Expanding
f^{(4)}
, we can see that all the terms will cap
f^{(14)}
, so this diagram is zero. If
R
also has a cap between strands 7 and 10, then it must have another cup. The only place a nonzero cup could happen would be between strands 4 and 5, which would give a partial trace of
f^{(4)}
. Recall that
E_{4}(f^{(4)})=\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}
. The
f^{(3)}
will still cap
f^{(14)}
, so this term is also zero.
∎

A symmetric argument gives the next lemma.

Lemma 4.22
.

In the planar algebra
\mathcal{P}
, the below diagram is zero.

\begin{overpic}[fig/3boxesand1belowstrandsonbottom.png]
\put(24.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(41.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(21.0,18.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(41.0,18.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(23.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(3.0,18.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(34.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\end{overpic}

Lemma 4.23
.

In the planar algebra, the below diagram is zero.

\begin{overpic}[fig/41strandsdown.png]
\put(5.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(23.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(42.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(60.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(34.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(3.0,17.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(22.0,17.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(52.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(42.0,17.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(60.0,18.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(32.5,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\end{overpic}

(18)

Proof.

Using equation
16
on the two left
S
-boxes gives that diagram in (
18
) equals

{{\sum_{i}}}a_{i}

\begin{overpic}[fig/311.png]
\put(20.0,25.0){$R_{i}$}
\put(23.0,40.0){$S$}
\put(42.0,40.0){$S$}

\put(32.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}

\put(22.0,32.0){\tiny{$8$}}
