\put(41.0,30.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(59.0,30.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(22.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\end{overpic}

-6{{\sum_{i}}}a_{i}

\begin{overpic}[fig/311.png]
\put(20.0,25.0){$R_{i}$}
\put(21.0,40.0){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(42.0,40.0){$S$}
\put(60.0,40.0){$S$}
\put(32.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(37.0,32.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(22.0,32.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(51.0,43.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(41.0,30.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(59.0,30.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(22.0,17.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\end{overpic}

where
R_{i}
has 8 strands on top and 10 strands on bottom and
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.
R_{i}
must have some cap.

Consider a diagram in the left sum. Since
S
is U.C.C.S., there must be a cap between strands 7 and 8. If the diagram is nonzero, there can be no other caps as then there will be another cup, which will cup
S
resulting in zero. Then the rest of the strands are straight and the resulting diagram is

\begin{overpic}[fig/3boxesand1belowstrandsonbottom.png]
\put(24.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(41.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(21.0,18.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(41.0,18.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(23.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(3.0,18.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(34.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\end{overpic}

which we already know is zero.

Consider a diagram in the right sum. Again, there must be a cap between strands 7 and 8. If the rest of the strands are straight then we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/3boxesand1belowstrandsonbottom.png]
\put(24.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,26.0){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(41.0,26.0){$S$}
\put(21.0,18.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(41.0,18.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(23.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(3.0,18.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(34.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\end{overpic}.

Expanding
f^{(4)}
will cap
f^{(14)}
, so this term is zero. If
R_{i}
had another cap, which, in order to be nonzero, would need to be between strands 6 and 7, then there must be a cup between strands 4 and 5. This gives a partial trace of
f^{(4)}
, which is a scalar multiple of
f^{(3)}
. Expanding
f^{(3)}
will cap
f^{(14)}
, giving zero.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

\mathcal{R}(Q_{4}\otimes X)f^{(10)}-2\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))f^{(10)}=0
.

Proof.

Since
f^{(10)}
is uncappable,
\mathcal{R}(Q_{4}\otimes X)f^{(10)}=0
. Recall that
Q_{4}=\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S
, so expanding the second term gives
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))f^{(10)}
equals

\displaystyle\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X))f^{(10)}+\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X))f^{(10)}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X))f^{(10)}+\frac{1}{25}\mathcal{R}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X))f^{(10)}

By Lemma
4.20
, the last term is zero. The other terms involve an
f^{(4)}
on top of an
f^{(10)}
which will cap it and become zero.

∎

Lemma 4.25
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
is uncuppable and uncappable.

Proof.

There are no strands on the top, so
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
is obviously uncappable. Putting a cup between strands 1 and 2, 2 and 3, 3 and 4, 7 and 8, 8 and 9, and 9 and 10 are all zero since they cup
Q_{4}
.

Recall from Lemma
4.3
that
E_{4}(Q_{4})=\frac{1}{2}f^{(3)}
. Putting a cup between strands 4 and 5 gives
\mathcal{R}(Q_{4})-2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(Q_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X))=\mathcal{R}((Q_{4})-\mathcal{R}(Q_{4}))=0
, since
Q_{4}
is uncuppable and uncappable. Nearly identically, a cup between strands 6 and 7 also gives zero. Putting a cup between strands 5 and 6 gives
2\mathcal{R}(Q_{4})-2\mathcal{R}((Q_{4})^{2})=0
. Thus
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
is also uncuppable.
∎

From this we now obtain a leaf relation.

Lemma 4.26
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
(Q_{4}\otimes X)-2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)=0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)-2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
is uncuppable and uncappable,
\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)-2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))=\mathcal{R}((Q_{4}\otimes X)-2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))f^{(10)}=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X))-2\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))
is uncuppable and uncappable.

Proof.

There are no strands at the top of the diagram so
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X))-2\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))
is uncappable. Since
P_{6}
is uncuppable, putting a cup between strands 1 and 2, 2 and 3, 3 and 4, 4 and 5, 5 and 6, 9 and 10, 10 and 11, 11 and 12, 12 and 13, and 13 and 14 gives zero.

Recall that
E_{6}(P_{6})=\frac{1}{2}P_{5}
. Putting a cup between strands 6 and 7 gives
\mathcal{R}(P_{6})-2\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(P_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X))=\mathcal{R}(P_{6})-\mathcal{R}(P_{6})=0
. An identical argument works for putting a cup between strands 8 and 9. Putting a cup between strands 7 and 8 gives
2\mathcal{R}(P_{6})-2\mathcal{R}((P_{6})^{2})=0
. Therefore,
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X))-2\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))
is uncuppable.
∎

Lemma 4.28
.

\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X))f^{(14)}-2\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))f^{(14)}=0
.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathcal{R}(P_{6}\otimes X)f^{(14)}=0
since the rainbowed
X
caps
f^{(14)}
. Recall that
P_{6}=P_{5}\otimes X-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)
. Using this, we expand the bottom
P_{6}\otimes X
to get that
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))f^{(14)}
equals

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2}))f^{(14)}-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}((P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2}))

The first term above is zero since
f^{(14)}
will be capped.

We now focus on computing the second term. We will first expand both
P_{6}
in
(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)
down to a linear combination of
S
and
f^{(4)}
. Along the way, we will cancel any term that, when rainbowed, will cap
f^{(14)}
. We will use the recursive absorption rule several times. Then
(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)
equals

\displaystyle(P_{6}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 5}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{5}(X^{\otimes 5}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})-\frac{3}{2}(P_{6}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 5}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{5}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X}\otimes X)(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})

The first term will cap
f^{(14)}
so we just need to continue expanding the second term. The
P_{6}
can absorb the top
P_{5}
. So the second term equals
-\frac{3}{2}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})
. Expanding
P_{5}
gives that
-\frac{3}{2}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})
equals

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})+2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

The first term will cap
f^{(14)}
when rainbowed over. When
P_{6}
absorbs the
P_{4}
we obtain
2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
for the second term. Using that
P_{4}=\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S
, we get that
2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
equals

\displaystyle\frac{6}{5}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})-\frac{2}{5}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

The first term, when rainbowed, will cap
f^{(14)}
when expanding the
f^{(4)}
. For the second term, we expand
P_{6}
to get that
-\frac{2}{5}(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
equals

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})+\frac{3}{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

When rainbowed on top of
f^{(14)}
, the first term will be zero. Then the second term can be expanded to equal

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})-\frac{4}{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{6}e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

The first term will cap
f^{(14)}
when rainbowed onto a
f^{(14)}
. The
P_{5}
in the second term can absorb
P_{4}
which will then be equal to
-\frac{4}{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
. Expanding
P_{4}
gives that
-\frac{4}{5}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
equals

\displaystyle-\frac{12}{25}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3}+\frac{4}{25}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

The first term, when expanding the
f^{(4)}
, will cap the
f^{(14)}
when rainbowed. For the second term, expanding
P_{5}
gives that
\frac{4}{25}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{5}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})
equals

\displaystyle\frac{4}{25}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})-\frac{16}{75}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

The first term, when rainbowed, will cap
f^{(14)}
and thus be zero. The second term can have each of the
P_{4}
expanded. This gives

\displaystyle-\frac{48}{625}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})+\frac{16}{625}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

\displaystyle+\frac{16}{625}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(f^{(4)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})-\frac{16}{1875}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}e_{5}e_{6}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{5}e_{4}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 3})

Consider each of these terms rainbowed over an
f^{(14)}
. Then these, up to scalar multiple, are of the form

\begin{overpic}[fig/41strandsdown.png]
\put(5.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(23.0,25.0){$R$}
\put(42.0,25.0){$T$}
\put(60.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(34.0,9.0){$f^{(14)}$}
\put(3.0,17.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(22.0,17.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(52.0,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(42.0,17.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(60.0,18.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(32.5,28.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\end{overpic}

where
R,T\in\{f^{(4)},S\}
. When both
R
and
T
are
S
, then Lemma
4.23
, the diagram is zero. When both
R
and
T
are
f^{(4)}
, we can see by expanding
f^{(4)}
that the only possible term not capping
f^{(14)}
has the form with
c=1
of Lemma
13
, which is zero. If exactly one of
R
or
S
is
f^{(4)}
then the only options of diagrams not capping
f^{(14)}
or
S
must be have a cap between strands 3 and 4 and a cup between strands 3 and 4, which will be a scalar multiple of the diagram from either Lemma
4.21
or
4.22
, which are both zero. Therefore,
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))f^{(14)}=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.29
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
(P_{6}\otimes X)-2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)=0
.

Proof.

We have that
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)-2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))f^{(14)}=0
and
\mathcal{R}((P_{6}\otimes X)-2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X))
is uncuppable and uncappable, which gives the result.
∎

4.2
Closed Diagram Computations in the Planar Algebra

Next, we compute a variety of diagrams in
\mathcal{P}
that will become beneficial to know later on.

Lemma 4.30
.

For
4<c<8
, the below diagram is zero.

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelow.png]
\put(28.0,17.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(-2.0,17.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(5.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,24.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(23.0,25.0){$S$}
\put(11.0,8.0){$f^{(16-2c)}$}
\end{overpic}

(19)

Proof.

Fix
4<c<8
. Since
c-4>0
, the two
S
boxes form an
S^{2}
-box with some cup, cap, or side cap. We can then expand this
S^{2}
-box by using that
S^{2}=6f^{(4)}+S
. Since
S
is U.C.C.S., the second term is zero. On the other hand, the first term is 6 times the following diagram (where the
f^{(4)}
is rainbowed with all endpoints on the bottom):

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxestopcupped.png]
\put(33.0,17.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(15.0,16.0){\tiny{$c-4$}}
\put(15.0,24.5){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(-3.0,17.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(11.0,6.5){$f^{(16-2c)}$}
\end{overpic}

When expanding
f^{(4)}
, every term will cap
f^{(16-2c)}
and thus will also be zero.
∎

Lemma 4.31
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
\text{tr}(S^{2})=30
.

Proof.

Since
S^{2}=S+6f^{(4)}
and
S
is U.C.C.S.,
\text{tr}(S^{2})=6\text{tr}(f^{(4)})=6\cdot 5=30
.
∎

Lemma 4.32
.

The following equality holds in
\mathcal{P}
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxesconnected.png]
\put(23.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(16.0,25.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(23.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,14.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(27.0,14.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(16.0,1.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\end{overpic}

=225

Proof.

First reduce the left set of vertical
S
-boxes connected by 5 strands using that
S^{2}=S+6f^{(4)}
. The term with an
S
-box will be zero since
S
is U.C.C.S. The other term will have a partial trace of
f^{(4)}
, which was calculated in Lemma
4.3
to be
\frac{5}{4}f^{(3)}
. As
S
is U.C.C.S.,
S
absorbs the
f^{(3)}
, resulting in
\frac{5}{4}\cdot 6\cdot\text{tr}(S^{2})=225
.
∎

Lemma 4.33
.

The following equality holds in
\mathcal{P}
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxesconnected.png]
\put(23.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(16.0,25.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(23.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,14.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(27.0,14.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(16.0,1.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\end{overpic}

=300

(20)

Proof.

First reduce the left set of vertical
S
-boxes using the
S^{2}
relation. The term with
S
will become zero as
S
is U.C.C.S. The other term will have the leftmost box as
E_{3}(E_{4}(f^{(4)}))=\frac{5}{4}\cdot\frac{4}{3}f^{(2)}=\frac{5}{3}f^{(2)}
by Lemma
4.3
. Since
S
is U.C.C.S., diagram (
20
) will equal
6\cdot\frac{5}{3}\text{tr}(S^{2})=300
.
∎

Lemma 4.34
.

The following equality holds in
\mathcal{P}
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxesconnected.png]
\put(23.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,4.0){$S$}
\put(5.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(16.0,25.0){\tiny{$1$}}
\put(23.0,22.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,14.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(27.0,14.0){\tiny{$7$}}
\put(16.0,1.0){\tiny{$1$}}
\end{overpic}

=450

(21)

Proof.

First reduce the left set of vertical
S
-boxes using the
S^{2}
relation. The term with
S
will become zero since
S
is U.C.C.S. The other term will have the leftmost box as
E_{2}(E_{3}(E_{4}(f^{(4)})))=\frac{5}{4}\cdot\frac{4}{3}\cdot\frac{3}{2}X=\frac{5}{2}X
by Lemma
4.3
. Then diagram (
21
) will equal
6\cdot\frac{5}{2}\text{tr}(S^{2})=450
.
∎

Lemma 4.35
.

The following equality holds in
\mathcal{P}
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxesoneinmiddle.png]
\put(24.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(6.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(6.0,38.0){$S$}
\put(16.0,40.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(23.0,38.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,14.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(28.0,14.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(16.0,1.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(3.0,30.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(28.0,30.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(15.0,22.0){$f^{(8)}$}
\end{overpic}

=30

Proof.

First, we use that
S^{2}=S+6f^{(4)}
to reduce the two top and two bottom
S
-boxes. All but one of the terms in this expansion will involve an
f^{(4)}
box on top or on bottom of
f^{(8)}
. All terms in the expansion of
f^{(4)}
will cup or cap the
f^{(8)}
box. Therefore, the only nonzero term is:

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbetween.png]
\put(13.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(13.0,38.0){$S$}
\put(10.0,14.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(10.0,29.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(12.0,21.0){$f^{(8)}$}
\end{overpic}

Then we can expand
f^{(8)}
using Frenkel and Khovanov’s result from Proposition
3.1
. All the terms with a cup will be zero since
S
is U.C.C.S. So only the first term in the expansion, which has coefficient 1 will be nonzero. This then results in
\text{tr}(S^{2})
which equals
30
.
∎

4.3
Trivalent Graphical Notation

In this section, we introduce the reader to notation from
[
15
]
and give some computations that will later be needed.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
a,b,
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. If
a+b-c
,
a+c-b
, and
b+c-a
are all positive and even, we say
(a,b,c)
is
admissible
. In this case, define

\displaystyle m\coloneq\frac{a+b-c}{2},\quad n\coloneq\frac{b+c-a}{2},\text{ and }\ell\coloneq\frac{a+c-b}{2}.

Definition 4.4
.

(Kauffman-Lins
[
15
]
, Chapter 4)
An unlabelled box in the
a
-box space is
f^{(a)}
. That is,

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07164pt{\raisebox{8.5359pt}{$f^{(a)}\coloneq$}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/box1strand.png]
\put(10.0,6.5){\tiny{$a$}}
\end{overpic}.

Suppose
(a,b,c)
is admissible. A trivalent vertex is defined as follows

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/trivalentvertex.png]
\put(2.0,9.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(0.0,3.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\end{overpic}{\raisebox{8.5359pt}{$\coloneq$}}\hskip 5.69054pt{\raisebox{-34.14322pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/expandedtrivalentvertex.png]
\put(24.0,30.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(1.0,5.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(52.0,6.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(18.0,20.0){\tiny{$m$}}
\put(35.0,15.0){\tiny{$\ell$}}
\put(24.0,6.0){\tiny{$n$}}
\end{overpic}}}.

In particular, this gives for
(a,b,k)
admissible that

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$k$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\end{overpic}\hskip 11.38109pt{\raisebox{8.5359pt}{$\coloneq$}}\hskip 5.69054pt{\raisebox{-22.76228pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/expandedtwotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(4.0,22.0){\tiny{$a$}}

\put(5.0,7.0){\tiny{$\ell$}}
\put(10.0,14.0){\tiny{$\ell$}}
\end{overpic}}}.

where the middle box is
f^{(k)}
.

Definition 4.5
.

(Kauffman-Lins
[
15
]
, Chapter 6)
Let
(a,b,c)
be admissible. Define:

\theta(a,b,c)\coloneq

\begin{overpic}[fig/theta.png]
\put(6.0,10.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(6.0,2.5){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(6.0,-2.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\end{overpic}

and

\displaystyle{\raisebox{22.76228pt}{$\text{Net}(m,n,\ell)\coloneq$}}\hskip 17.07164pt\begin{overpic}[fig/Net.png]
\put(24.0,30.0){\tiny{$\ell$}}
\put(42.0,20.0){\tiny{$n$}}
\put(15.0,20.0){\tiny{$m$}}
\end{overpic}.

The following equalities were found in
[
15
]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

(Kauffman-Lins
[
15
]
, Lemma 8 and Corollary 2)
Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
where
(a,b,c)
is admissible and define
m,n,
and
\ell
as in Definition
4.3
. Then
\theta(a,b,c)=Net(m,n,\ell)
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\text{Net}(m,n,\ell)=\frac{(-1)^{m+n+\ell}[m]![n]![\ell]![m+n+\ell+1]!}{[m+n]![n+\ell]![m+\ell]!}

In particular, when
m+n
is even,
\text{Net}(m,n,0)=[m+n+1]
.

Define
{{\sum_{k=r}^{s}}}{\hskip-8.5359pt\tiny{2}}\hskip 8.5359ptf(k)\coloneq f(r)+f(r+2)+...+f(s-2)+f(s)
. Then the following was proven by Chen in
[
6
]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

(Chen
[
6
]
, Theorem 3.3.5) For
a,b\geq 0
,

\displaystyle f^{(a)}\otimes f^{(b)}={{\sum_{k=|a-b|}}^{a+b}}{\hskip-17.07182pt\tiny{2}}\hskip 14.22636pt\left(\frac{[k+1]}{|\theta(a,b,k)|}\hskip 2.84526pt{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$k$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\end{overpic}}}\right)

Remark
.

Recall from Lemma
3.1
that
[n]=n
for index 4 planar algebras.

Lemma 4.36
.

We can calculate the following
\theta
-values.

(i)

|\theta(5,5,0)|=6
,

(ii)

|\theta(5,5,2)|=\frac{21}{5}
,

(iii)

|\theta(5,5,4)|=\frac{14}{5}
,

(iv)

|\theta(5,5,6)|=\frac{63}{25}
,

(v)

|\theta(5,5,8)|=\frac{18}{5}
, and

(vi)

|\theta(5,5,10)|=11
.

Proof.

In all parts
a=b=5
. When
c=0
, this gives
m=5
,
n=0
, and
\ell=0
. Thus
|\theta(5,5,0)|=|\text{Net}(5,0,0)|=6
. We calculate the other values of
\theta
based on their
c
-value below.

Value of
c

(m,n,\ell)

|\text{Net}(m,n,l)|

2

(4,1,1)

\frac{4!1!1!7!}{5!2!5!}=\frac{21}{5}

4

(3,2,2)

\frac{3!2!2!8!}{5!5!4!}=\frac{14}{5}

6

(2,3,3)

\frac{2!3!3!9!}{5!5!6!}=\frac{63}{25}

8

(1,4,4)

\frac{1!4!4!10!}{5!5!8!}=\frac{18}{5}

10

(0,5,5)

\frac{0!5!5!11!}{5!5!10!}=11
,

as we wished.
∎

4.4
The Jellyfish Algorithm

We now give a jellyfish algorithm for evaluating closed diagrams in
\mathcal{P}
. See figure
13
for a schematic illustration. Later, we will show this algorithm is a well-defined function onto
\mathbb{C}
. This will show that the 0-box space is one-dimensional.

Let
J:\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function defined on diagrams in the following way. Let
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

1.

If
D
has no
S
-boxes, skip to step 7(iv) and use
D=D_{i}
.

2.

Assume
D
has
m\geq 1

S
-boxes. Make
m
evenly spaced points at the top of the diagram of
D
, starting with a point at the left side and ending at the right side. Label the points
1,...,m
.

3.

Pick an
S
-box and label is
S_{1}
. From the distinguished star of
S_{1}
to the point 1 at the top of
D
, draw an imaginary arc only traversing strands (the arc cannot pass through a crossing or box).

4.

Drag
S_{1}
by stretching its strands along the imaginary arc, going under strands it passes along the way.

5.

Repeat steps 3 and 4
m
times for the rest of the
S
boxes and points.

6.

Resolve all the crossings, which may make
D
as sum of diagrams, say
D=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\ell}}a_{i}D_{i}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

7.

Look individually at each
D_{i}
.

(i)

If
D_{i}
has any
S
-box that is cupped, define
J(D_{i})=0
.

(ii)

If
D_{i}
has no
S
-box that is cupped, then there must be a pair of adjacent
S
-boxes connected by 4 strands. Use the
S^{2}
relation to rewrite
D_{i}
as a linear combination of diagrams with at least one less
S
-box.

(iii)

Repeat steps 7(i) and 7(ii) on all summands resulting from
D_{i}
until no
S
-boxes are remaining.

(iv)

Now each summand resulting from
D
is in Temperley-Lieb. Define
J(D_{i})
to be its evaluation in
\mathcal{TL}
.

Extend the function linearly to be defined on all of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

\put(62.0,46.0){$S$}
\put(42.0,41.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 13
:
Schematic illustration of the jellyfish algorithm for the affine
E7
planar algebra (viewed in a circular tank).

We focus this section on proving the below theorem.

Theorem 4.39
.

The map
J:\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
is well-defined, i.e.,

(i)

the evaluation algorithm does not depend on the order of
S^{2}
to reduce in step 7(ii),

(ii)

the algorithm does not depend on the choice of arc in step 3,

(iii)

the algorithm does not depend on the order of the
S
-boxes chosen to float to the top in step 3, and

(iv)

the algorithm respects the defining relations.

Remark
.

At step 7 of the jellyfish algorithm, for each summand, we have all the
S
-boxes at the top of the tank and strands with no intersections on the bottom. Without loss of generality, we can instead assume our tank is a circle. Then, for example, we can view the below diagram as being the two top
S
-boxes adjacent in the circle and the rest of diagram being at the bottom of the circle, where
T
is in Temperley-Lieb. If instead the jellyfish algorithm has only been completed up to step 5, we can assume that there are crossings in
T
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxesoneinmiddle.png]
\put(24.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(6.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(6.0,38.0){$S$}
\put(23.0,38.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,14.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(28.0,14.0){\tiny{$d$}}
\put(3.0,30.0){\tiny{$a$}}
\put(28.0,30.0){\tiny{$b$}}
\put(14.0,21.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

4.5
The Jellyfish Algorithm: Order of
S^{2}
.

Lemma 4.40
.

The jellyfish algorithm is well defined with respect to the order in reducing
S^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
D
be a diagram in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and suppose the jellyfish algorithm has been completed for
D
up through step 6. Consider
D_{i}
from the summand created in step 6. We will prove the jellyfish algorithm is well defined with respect to the order in reducing
S^{2}
by induction on
n
, the number of
S
-boxes in
D_{i}
at this point.

If
D_{i}
has 0, 1, or 2
S
-boxes, then there is no choice in the order of reduction using
S^{2}
, so the base case is true. Suppose that for some
n\geq 2
, if
D_{i}
has
n

S
-boxes, the choice in the order of reducing
S^{2}
does not matter. We want to show that for
1\leq j<k\leq\ell<p\leq n+1
, if
\{S_{k},S_{k}\}
and
\{S_{\ell},S_{p}\}
both form an
S^{2}
, reducing
\{S_{j},S_{k}\}
first is the same number as reducing
\{S_{\ell},S_{p}\}
first. (Notice that this covers the
n=3
case since we allow
k=\ell
).

We prove in cases. Case 1 is assuming that
k=\ell
. That is, we want to show that first reducing
\{S_{j},S_{k}\}
evaluates to the same as first reducing
\{S_{k},S_{p}\}
. These boxes must all be adjacent, so at Step 6, we have the following picture:

\begin{overpic}[fig/3boxesand1below.png]
\put(24.0,20.0){$S_{k}$}
\put(5.0,20.0){$S_{j}$}
\put(41.0,20.0){$S_{p}$}
\put(21.0,12.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(41.0,12.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(25.0,3.0){$T$}
\put(3.0,12.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(34.0,22.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(15.0,22.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\end{overpic}

where
T
is the rest of the diagram. When we first reduce
\{S_{j},S_{k}\}
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelowclosed.png]
\put(28.0,12.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(2.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(5.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,19.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(23.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,3.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 5.69054pt{\raisebox{28.45274pt}{$+6$}}\hskip 5.69054pt\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelowclosed.png]
\put(28.0,12.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(2.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(4.0,19.0){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(15.0,19.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(23.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,3.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}.

We now have two diagrams with at least one less
S
-box, so the choice in the order of reduction from here on out does not matter. Continuing step 7, we can notice that in the second summand, since
S
is U.C.C.S.,
S
will absorb
f^{(4)}
. Then we can reduce the above diagram to

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesstacked.png]
\put(12.0,20.0){$S^{2}$}
\put(17.0,11.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(13.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

+6

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesstacked.png]
\put(12.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(17.0,11.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(13.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

This is identical to what is obtained if instead
\{S_{k},S_{p}\}
is first reduced. The rest of the algorithm will then be identical for both cases.

In case 2, assume
k\neq\ell
. Then at step 6 we have the following picture:

\begin{overpic}[fig/4boxesinpairs1below.png]
\put(5.0,20.0){$S_{j}$}
\put(23.0,20.0){$S_{k}$}
\put(42.0,20.0){$S_{\ell}$}
\put(60.0,20.0){$S_{p}$}
\put(34.0,3.0){$T$}
\put(3.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(15.0,22.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(22.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(52.0,22.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(42.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(60.0,11.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(32.5,22.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}

Reducing
\{S_{i},S_{j}\}
first gives

6

\begin{overpic}[fig/3boxesin1pairand1below.png]

Each of these components have one less

-box so the next choice to reduce in the pairing does not matter. So we choose next to pair

and obtain

This will be the same result as first choosing to pair
\{S_{\ell},S_{p}\}
, so the choice in the order of reducing
S^{2}
where
j\neq k
doesn’t matter either.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

It is now clear that if a diagram in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
has been plugged into the jellyfish algorithm and the first six steps have been performed, then if one can evaluate the resulting diagram by only using the
S^{2}
relation, the bubble relation, and that
S
is U.C.C.S., then the evaluation of the diagram in
\mathcal{P}
will equal its evaluation for the jellyfish algorithm. In particular, this gives that Lemmas
4.31
,
4.32
,
4.33
,
4.34
, and
4.35
are also giving the evaluation of those diagrams in the jellyfish algorithm. Lemma
4.30
also only uses the
S^{2}
relation and that
S
is U.C.C.S., so it is clear that if at step 7 of the jellyfish algorithm we have this diagram in some local neighborhood, then it will evaluate to zero.

4.6
The Jellyfish Algorithm: Choice of Arc

Next we show that the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the choice of arc.

Definition 4.6
.

We define the follow local relations involving imaginary arcs (drawn in red) and strands of the planar algebra (black strands).

\begin{aligned} &{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\mathbf{\Omega_{1}}:$}}}\hskip 8.53581pt\begin{overpic}[fig/redstrand.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\leftrightarrow$}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/redreid1.png]
\end{overpic}\\
&{\raisebox{5.69046pt}{{$\mathbf{\Omega_{2}}:$}}}\hskip 8.53581pt\begin{overpic}[fig/redXtensorX.png]
\end{overpic}\quad{\raisebox{11.38092pt}{{$\leftrightarrow$}}}\quad\begin{overpic}[fig/redreid2.png]
\end{overpic}\end{aligned}

\mathbf{\Omega_{3}}:

\begin{overpic}[fig/redreid3a.png]
\end{overpic}

\leftrightarrow

\begin{overpic}[fig/redreid3b.png]
\end{overpic}

\mathbf{\Omega_{4}}:

\begin{overpic}[fig/redstrandsoverbox.png]
\put(8.0,8.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}

\leftrightarrow

\begin{overpic}[fig/redstrandoverboxstrands.png]

\end{overpic}

Remark
.

The proof of Reidemeister’s theorem
[
27
]
can be adapated to show all imaginary arcs with the same fixed endpoints are connected by a finite sequence of
\Omega
-moves.

Lemma 4.41
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{1}
moves.

Proof.

Fix an
S
-box and a point
k
on the top of the diagram created in step 2 of the jellyfish algorithm. Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two imaginary arcs identical except in a neighborhood where
\gamma_{1}
is the left-hand side of the
\Omega_{1}
move and
\gamma_{2}
is the right-hand side of
\Omega_{1}
. Then when dragging the
S
-box along
\gamma_{1}
we have

\begin{overpic}[fig/Sbox1strand.png]
\put(2.0,4.5){$S$}
\end{overpic}

\mapsto

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxextendedstrand.png]
\put(2.0,7.5){$S$}
\end{overpic}

while dragging the
S
-box along
\gamma_{2}
gives

\begin{overpic}[fig/Sbox1strand.png]
\put(2.0,4.5){$S$}
\end{overpic}

\mapsto

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxomega1.png]
\put(6.5,13.0){$S$}
\put(1.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}

Thus, to show the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{1}
boils down to showing the below to diagrams evaluate the same in the jellyfish algorithm for any
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,X^{\otimes 8})
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesstacked.png]
\put(17.0,11.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(13.0,18.5){$S$}
\put(13.0,3.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt,\hskip 5.69054ptand\hskip 8.53581pt\begin{overpic}[fig/boxomega1box.png]
\put(32.0,37.0){$S$}
\put(21.0,12.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(25.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

(22)

Assume that up through step 5 of the jellyfish algorithm has been completed. Suppose that at the current stage, the diagram looks like the right-hand side of the above diagram. The next step is to resolve crossings. Use Lemma
4.11
to resolve the innermost loop. This multiplies the diagram by
i
. Then use Lemma
4.12
to uncross the rightmost strand of
S
crossed over the other 7 strands of
S
. This multiplies the diagram by
i^{7}
. Now the diagram is

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxomega1box1resolve.png]
\put(32.0,37.0){$S$}
\put(21.0,12.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(25.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

Repeating this for the seven other twists, we obtain the diagram on the left of (
22
), which gives the result.
∎

Lemma 4.42
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{2}
moves.

Proof.

Fix an
S
-box and a point on the boundary. Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two imaginary arcs from the
S
-box’s star to the point on the boundary such that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are identical except in a neighborhood where
\gamma_{1}
locally looks like the left-hand side of
\Omega_{2}
and
\gamma_{2}
looks like the right-hand side. Complete steps 4 and 5 of the jellyfish algorithm identically in both cases. These two cases are identical except in a local neighborhood. In step 6, apply Reidemeister II to make the neighborhoods identical.
∎

The same idea for proof as above except using Reidemeister III proves the following.

Lemma 4.43
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{3}
moves.

Lemma 4.44
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under choice of arc.

Proof.

By the previous lemmas, all that is left to show is that the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{4}
moves. I will show this by induction on the number of
S
-boxes left to drag to the top.

Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be the same diagram in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Suppose we have taken equivalent steps of the jellyfish algorithm for
D
and
D^{\prime}
until there is one
S
-box left to drag to the top. For
D
choose arc
\gamma_{1}
to drag the final
S
-box to the top and for
D^{\prime}
choose
\gamma_{2}
. These two arcs are connected by a finite sequence of
\Omega
-moves. Since we already know the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
, and
\Omega_{3}
moves, we can assume that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are equivalent up to some
\Omega_{4}
moves. That is, suppose
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are equivalent except in a neighborhood
\gamma_{1}
looks like the left-hand side of the
\Omega_{4}
move and
\gamma_{2}
looks like the right-hand side, where the
S
-box is the same in both diagrams. However, as all but the last
S
-box has been floated to the top, this boils down to showing that the two diagrams in (
22
) evaluate the same in the jellyfish algorithm, which was already done in Lemma
4.41
. Thus the base case has been proven.

Next, suppose that if
D
and
D^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
are the same diagram and we have taken equivalent steps of the jellyfish algorithm until at most
k
boxes are left to drag to the top, then different choices of arc for the rest of the
S
-boxes results in equivalent evaluations of the jellyfish algorithm for
D
and
D^{\prime}
.

Now suppose that
D
and
D^{\prime}
are equal diagrams in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and we have taken equivalent steps of the jellyfish algorithm until
k+1

S
-boxes are left to drag to the top. Choose the same
S
-box in
D
and
D^{\prime}
that will be dragged to point
m-k
at the top of the diagrams. For
D
, choose arc
\gamma_{1}
. For
D^{\prime}
choose arc
\gamma_{2}
. By the previous lemmas, we can assume
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are equivalent up to some
\Omega_{4}
move in an neighborhood. Suppose in this neighborhood
\gamma_{1}
looks like the left side of the
\Omega_{4}
and
\gamma_{2}
looks like the right side of the
\Omega_{4}
move.

Case 1:
The
S
-box in this neighborhood is the same as the
S
-box being dragged to the top. Then, using the same argument as done in the base case,
D
and
D^{\prime}
will evaluate to the same number in the jellyfish algorithm.

Case 2:
The
S
-box in the neighborhood is not the same as the
S
-box being dragged to the top. Call the
S
box being dragged
S_{m-k}
. As the first
m-k-1

S
-boxes are at the top already, the
S
-box in this case has not yet been dragged to the top. Call this box
S_{i}
. Finish dragging the
S_{m-k}
to the top. By induction, the rest of the
S
-boxes being dragged to the top can use any choice of arc. Except for
S_{i}
, choose the arcs dragging the rest of the
S
-boxes to avoid the local neighborhood where the
\Omega_{4}
move was done for
S_{m-k}
. When dragging
S_{i}
to the top, choose an imaginary arc that will pass through the neigbhorhood once. So, outside of this neighborhood, both
D
and
D^{\prime}
are identical. Inside the neighborhood for
D
,
S_{m-k}
was dragged under strands for
S_{i}
. For
D^{\prime}
,
S_{i}
was dragged under the strands for
S_{m-k}
. Therefore, at step 6,
D
and
D^{\prime}
are identical except in a neighborhood where one looks like the left diagram below and other looks like the right diagram below.

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.5}{ \begin{overpic}[fig/neighborhood1.png]
\put(3.0,20.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(3.0,19.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(3.0,18.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(15.0,18.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}
\hskip 5.69054pt},\scalebox{0.5}{\hskip 5.69054pt
\begin{overpic}[fig/neighborhood2.png]
\put(3.0,20.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(3.0,19.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(3.0,18.0){\tiny{$.$}}
\put(18.0,18.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}},

where there are both 8 vertical and horizontal strands. In step 6 we resolve crossings. The two neighborhoods above vary by an even number of crossings. Therefore, by Lemma
4.15
, these two diagrams will be equivalent after completing step 6.
∎

4.7
The Jellyfish Algorithm: Order of Floating
S
-boxes

Lemma 4.45
.

Fix some
c\in\{0,...,8\}
and
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,X^{\otimes 2(8-c)})
. Assume the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up to step 5 on two diagram, which only differ as shown below in (
23
). Similarly, assume the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up to step 5 on two diagrams which only differ as shown below in (
24
). Then following steps 6 and 7 on each of the diagrams in (
23
) results in the same number if and only if following steps 6 and 7 on each of the diagrams in (
24
) results in the same number.

\begin{overpic}[fig/twistwithnobottomstrands.png]
\put(10.0,34.0){$S$}
\put(29.0,34.0){$S$}
\put(10.0,13.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(20.0,3.0){$T$}
\put(20.0,12.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(19.0,13.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\put(39.0,13.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt,\hskip 11.38109pt\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelowclosed.png]
\put(28.0,12.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(-2.0,11.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(5.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,19.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(23.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,3.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

(23)

\begin{overpic}[fig/twistwithnobottomstrands2.png]
\put(10.0,50.0){$S$}
\put(29.0,50.0){$S$}
\put(10.0,29.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(24.0,3.0){$T$}
\put(20.0,29.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(19.0,30.0){\tiny{$(c)$}}
\put(39.0,29.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(12.0,12.0){\tiny{$16-2c$}}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt,\hskip 11.38109pt\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxestwoontop2bigonbottom.png]
\put(14.0,4.0){$T$}
\put(5.0,35.0){$S$}
\put(24.0,35.0){$S$}
\put(-2.0,26.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(15.0,34.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(20.0,12.0){\tiny{$16-2c$}}
\put(28.0,26.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\end{overpic}

(24)

Proof.

The forward direction is clear. For the backwards direction, suppose that the jellyfish algorithm evaluates the two diagrams in (
24
) to be the same numbers. First suppose
c=8
. In the case the result is clear since the Jones-Wenzl projection is
f^{(0)}=\emptyset
. So the diagrams in (
24
) are equivalent to the diagrams in (
23
).

Suppose the result is true down to some
c+1
. Consider the case with
c
twisted strands and suppose the diagrams in (
24
) evaluate to the same number in the jellyfish algorithm. Expand
f^{(16-2c)}=X^{\otimes(16-2c)}+D
, where
D
is a linear combination of diagrams with a cup and cap somewhere. For each diagram, since
S
is U.C.C.S., the only nonzero terms in this expansion will have a cup in the
8-c
and
8-c+1
position, which are a sum of terms from the same diagram with
c+k
strands in the middle where
k\geq 1
. The jellyfish algorithm is clearly linear, so by induction, the equivalence from the
c+k
case for
k\geq 1
will imply the equivalence of terms in the
c
case.
∎

A symmetric argument will show the following.

Lemma 4.46
.

Fix
c\in\{0,...,8\}
and
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,X^{\otimes 2(8-c)})
. Assume the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up to step 5 on two diagrams which only differ as shown below in (

). Similarly, assume the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up to step 5 on two diagrams which only differ as shown below in (

). Then following steps 6 and 7 on each of the diagrams in (

) results in the same number if and only if following steps 6 and 7 on each of the diagrams in (

) results in the same number.

(26)

We now focus on proving that the jellyfish algorithm evaluates the diagrams in (

) and (

) equivalently by going through all the possible cases.

Lemma 4.47
.

Assume the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up through step 5 (so the “circular" diagram may have crossings at the bottom). If at this point, the diagram is of the form:

\begin{overpic}[fig/fourboxestwoontop2bigonbottom.png]
\put(14.0,4.0){$T$}
\put(5.0,35.0){$S$}
\put(24.0,35.0){$S$}
\put(3.0,26.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(15.0,34.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(18.0,11.0){\tiny{$10$}}
\put(28.0,26.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(14.0,19.0){$f^{(10)}$}
\end{overpic}

(27)

where
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,X^{\otimes 10})
, and
T
may have crossings, then the jellyfish algorithm will evaluate this diagram to zero.

Proof.

We begin with step 6 of the jellyfish algorithm. It is well known in knot theory that the order in which we resolve crossings does not matter. So suppose all crossings of
T
are now resolved and without loss of generality redefine
T
to be any such summand in this resolution. As
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,f^{(10)})
, we know that if
T\in\mathcal{TL}
then it would cup
f^{(10)}
. Steps 7(i) through (iii) will not change the cupped
f^{(10)}
, so in step 7(iv), we get that the evaluation is zero.

Now suppose that
T
has exactly one
S
-box. As
f^{(10)}
and
T
are connected by 10 strands, there still must be a cup somewhere on
f^{(10)}
and again the diagram will evaluate to zero.

Suppose
T
has exactly two
S
-boxes. Using Lemma
4.40
, we can perform steps 7(i) through (iii) for the
S
-boxes in
T
. If there is some
S^{2}
, we reduce to the previous case. Otherwise, we can assume that
T
has two
S
-boxes are connected by at most 3 strands. Then, as there are 10 points on the boundary, either some
S
-box is capped and thus by step 7(i) will evaluate the diagram to zero, or
T
is, up to multiplication by a scalar, the below diagram,

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedup.png]
\put(23.0,5.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,8.0){\tiny{$3$}}
\put(5.0,5.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}.

(28)

Suppose that
T
has
n

S
-boxes. By Lemma
4.40
, we can assume that we have used steps 7(i) and (ii) so that no
S^{2}
-boxes are remaining inside
T
. Then the leftmost
S
box has at most 3 strands connected to the adjacent
S
-box and at least 5 strands on the boundary. Similarly, the rightmost
S
-box has at most 3 strands connected to its neighbor and at least 5 strands on the boundary. Therefore, with the leftmost and rightmost
S
-boxes, we have accounted for all the strands on the boundary of
T
. If there are other
S
-boxes between the rightmost and leftmost
S
-boxes, there must be one connected only to its neighbors, forming an
S^{2}
, which contradicts that no
S^{2}
remains in
T
. Therefore, the only case to consider is when
T
is a scalar multiple of the diagram in (
28
). We compute the diagram in (
27
) using
T
equal to the diagram in (
28
).

We use Chen’s formula from Propostion
4.2
to get a formula for
f^{(5)}\otimes f^{(5)}
. Using the
\theta
-values found in Lemma
4.36
we obtain

f^{(5)}\otimes f^{(5)}=\frac{1}{6}\hskip 2.84526pt{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$0$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\end{overpic}}}+\frac{5}{7}{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$2$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\end{overpic}}}+\frac{25}{14}{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$4$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\end{overpic}}}+\frac{25}{9}{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(6.0,7.0){\tiny{$6$}}
\put(8.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\end{overpic}}}+\frac{5}{2}{\raisebox{-14.22636pt}{\begin{overpic}[fig/twotrivalentvertices.png]
\put(1.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(8.0,11.0){\tiny{$5$}}
\put(1.0,3.0){\tiny{$5$}}

Lemma 4.55
.

The jellyfish algorithm evaluates the two diagrams in (
24
) equivalently for all
c\in\{0,...,8\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that the jellyfish algorithm has been completed up through step 5 for two diagrams which at this point of the jellyfish algorithm look like the diagrams from (
24
). Recall that the proof of Lemma
4.18
only relied on resolving crossing. So by Lemma
4.18
, when
c
is even this lemma is true.

When
c
is odd, Lemma
4.18
gives that the diagrams in (
24
) evaluate to be the negatives of each other in the jellyfish algorithm. We show that the diagrams evaluate to zero. Continuing onto step 7 of the jellyfish algorihtm, we can assume that all
S^{2}
boxes along the top and along the bottom have been reduced. Therefore, only
c=1
and
c=3
cases need to be considered. However, when
c=3
and
c=1
, Lemmas
4.47
,
4.48
,
4.51
,
4.52
,
4.53
,
4.54
, give that the jellyfish algorithm evaluates to zero, giving the result.
∎

The same proof can be used for the below lemma.

Lemma 4.56
.

The jellyfish algorithm evaluates the two diagrams in (
26
) equivalently for all
c\in\{0,...,8\}
.

4.45
and
4.46

Lemma 4.57
.

The jellyfish algorithm evaluates the diagrams in (
23
) to be the same number.

Lemma 4.58
.

The jellyfish algorithm evaluates the diagrams in (
25
) to be the same number.

Lemma 4.59
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the order of the
S
-boxes chosen to drag to the top.

Proof.

The symmetric group is generated by transpositions of the form
(i\text{ }i+1)
, so it is enough to prove that the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under switching the
S
-box getting dragged to position
i
with the
S
-box getting dragged to position
i+1
.

Consider a closed diagram
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
with
m

S
-boxes. We want to compute the jellyfish algorithm in two ways. The first way drags an
S
-box, say
S_{i}
to point
i
at the top and an
S
-box,
S_{i+1}
to point
i+1
. The other way drags the
S_{i}
box to point
i+1
and
S_{i+1}
to point
i
. All other steps of the jellyfish algorithm up to this point are identical.

It has already been proven that the choice of arc doesn’t matter. There exists some neighborhood of the point
i
and
i+1
at the top where there are no strands. Call this neighborhood
N
. So in the first case, choose any arc,
\gamma_{i}
to drag from
S_{i}
to point
i
. For
S_{i+1}
, first drag
S_{i+1}
to the position where
S_{i}
’s star originally was, then follow the same path as
\gamma_{i}
up to the top until the
S_{i+1}
box is inside the neighborhood
N
, then complete
\gamma_{i+1}
by making an arc passing no more strands to the point
i+1
. (Technically,
\gamma_{i+1}
should always be on the right, within some local neighborhood of
\gamma_{i}
that crosses the same exactly strands as
\gamma_{i}
, since strands must cross transversely).

In the second case, make an imaginary arc
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}
from the star of
S_{i+1}
that follows
\gamma_{i+1}
until the neighborhood
N
, then follow the arc
\gamma_{i}
to the point
i
. Drag
S_{i+1}
to point
i
using this arc. Then make arc
\gamma_{i+1}^{\prime}
from the star of
S_{i}
following
\gamma_{i}
until the neighborhood
N
, then follow
\gamma_{i+1}
to the point
i+1
.

Showing that the first and second case are equivalent are is thus equivalent to showing that for all
c
where
0\leq c\leq 8
, the jellyfish algorithm evaluates the two diagrams in (
23
) to the same number (or the two diagrams in (
25
)). However, this was done in Lemmas
4.57
and
4.58
, so this proof is complete.
∎

4.8
The Jellyfish Algorithm: Respecting the Defining Relations

The last step in showing the jellyfish algorithm is a well-defined function is to show it respects the defining relations.

Lemma 4.60
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the bubble relation.

Proof.

Let
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
be some closed diagram with
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

S
-boxes and somewhere a bubble. Let
D^{\prime}
be the identical to
D
except without the bubble and instead multiplied by 2. As the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the choice of arc, do steps 1 through 7(iii) identically and for the imaginary arcs, do not go through the bubble. In step 7(iv) pop the bubble in
D
for a factor of 2. Now
D
and
D^{\prime}
are identical and will give the same number in the jellyfish algorithm.
∎

Lemma 4.61
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant with respect to the
S
is U.C.C.S. relation.

Proof.

Suppose
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
is a closed diagram with some
S
-box that is capped, cupped, or sidecapped on the right. Suppose that this box is dragged to position
i
at the top the diagram during the jellyfish algorithm. Then, since the jellyfish algorithm stretches strands up to the point, we get that at the end of step 5, we will have the
S_{i}
at the top with a cap on it that is still in the same position. Use Reidemeister moves to move any strands intersecting the cap. Then at step 7(i) this diagram will evaluate to zero.

If instead
S
is sidecapped on the right, then we are in the case of the below

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/boxoverstrand.png]
\put(6.0,6.2){$S$}
\put(6.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}.

Call the open neighborhood ball that looks like the above picture
N
. In this case, since the order in dragging
S
boxes to the top doesn’t matter, choose this box to be
S_{1}
. Choose the imaginary arc
\gamma_{1}
to 1 that is a straight line to the boundary of
N
, then goes to the point 1 by never re-entering the neighborhood. Then, when
S_{1}
reaches the top it will locally look like the below.

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxtwistsonbottom.png]
\put(4.0,6.2){$S$}
\put(4.0,1.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}.

(39)

Finish step 5 by dragging the rest of the
S
boxes up, avoiding the neighborhood that looks like the above. In step 6 resolve the crossings. Using Lemmas
4.11
and
4.12
we get that the above local neighborhood will resolve the crossing to have a cap on an
S
. Thus
D
will get sent to zero by the jellyfish algorithm.
∎

Lemma 4.62
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the
S^{2}
relation.

Proof.

Let
D\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
be a closed diagram with two
S
-boxes forming an
S^{2}
. Let
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
be a sum of two closed diagrams where they are identical to
D
except that in a local neighborhood,
B
, the
S^{2}
has been replaced by an
S
-box in one and 6 times
f^{(4)}
in the other. Make a point on the boundary of
B
. Suppose that
D
has
m-1
boxes and
D^{\prime}
has
m
boxes. There is a neighborhood,
N
, in
\mathcal{D}
containing points
m
and
m-1
at the top of all the diagrams that contains no strands. As the order of the
S
-boxes doesn’t matter, we can choose to drag the the two
S
-boxes in
\mathcal{D}
to the last two positions,
m-1
and
m
. When dragging the first
m-2

S
-boxes in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, we choose identical imaginary arcs that avoid the open ball
B
, the distinguished point on the boundary of
B
, and the neighborhood
N
.

As the jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the choice of arc, we can first choose the arc
\gamma_{m-1}
that is a straight line inside of the open ball until it hits the designated point. Then choose any arc from there to the point
m-1
that never re-enters the open ball. For the arc
\gamma_{m}
, make a straight line from the star of the second
S
-box to the designated point on the boundary. Then follow the path of
\gamma_{m-1}
until it reaches the neighborhood
N
, then go to the point
m
by staying inside the neighborhood
N
.

For the summand of
D^{\prime}
with the single
S
-box in
B
, choose this box to go the
m-1
position. In this case, this is the last point on the top of the diagram. For the arc
\gamma_{m-1}
, first choose an arc that goes from the star of
S
to the designated point of the ball
B
, staying within the ball, and then follow the arc of
\gamma_{m-1}
to the point
m-1
. Now the jellyfish algorithm can be assumed to be done for
D^{\prime}
up through step 5. In step 6, we can perform planar isotopies for the parts of the diagram in Temperley-Lieb. So for the
D^{\prime}
summand containing
6f^{(4)}
, first isotope the
f^{(4)}
using the same arc as the other summand for
D^{\prime}
to the top of the diagram.

For
D
, we now have a diagram of the below form where
c\geq 4
and
T\in\text{Hom}(\emptyset,X^{\otimes(16-2c})
(and
T
may include crossings).:

\displaystyle\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesconnectedoneboxbelowclosed.png]
\put(28.0,12.0){\tiny{$$8-c}}
\put(0.0,11.0){\tiny{$8-c$}}
\put(5.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,19.0){\tiny{$c$}}
\put(23.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(15.0,3.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}\hskip 2.84526pt.

On the other hand, the jellyfish form of
D^{\prime}
is

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesstacked.png]
\put(12.0,20.0){$S$}
\put(17.0,11.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(13.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

+6

\begin{overpic}[fig/twoboxesstacked.png]
\put(12.0,20.0){$f^{(4)}$}
\put(17.0,11.0){\tiny{$8$}}
\put(13.0,4.0){$T$}
\end{overpic}

Complete steps 6 through 7(i) identically for
D
and the summands of
D^{\prime}
. At step 7(ii) we can reduce the
S^{2}
in any order we choose. In
D
, first reduce the
S^{2}
at points
m
and
m-1
, which then will give the identical sum of diagrams in
D^{\prime}
. Therefore, the jellyfish algorithm will evaluate
D
and
D^{\prime}
to be the same number.
∎

Lemma 4.63
.

The jellyfish algorithm is invariant under the jellyfish relation.

Proof.

Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be closed diagrams in
\mathcal{P}_{0}
except in some neighborhood,
N
,
D
has the left side of the jellyfish relation (an
S
box underneath a strand) and
D^{\prime}
has the right side of the jellyfish relation (an
S
-box with a strand crossed over its strands beneath it). Pick a distinguished point on the boundary of
N
.

Begin the jellyfish algorithm identically for
D
and
D^{\prime}
, avoiding having imaginary arcs going through the neighborhood
N
. Now consider the
S
-box in the neighborhood
N
, for
D
choose a straight line from the star of the
S
box, through the overstand to the distinguished point of
N
, then a path outside of
N
to the point on the boundary that does not re-enter
N
. Choose the same imaginary arc for
D^{\prime}
. Notice now, when dragging the
S
-box in diagram
D
, once it passes under the overstrand inside of
N
, it looks identical to the diagram
D^{\prime}
. Now the diagrams are equal and the jellyfish algorithm will evaluate them to the same number.
∎

Theorem
4.39
.

We showed the choice in reducing
S^{2}
doesn’t matter in Lemma
4.40
. We showed the choice of arc doesn’t matter in Lemma
4.44
. We showed that the order of choosing the
S
-boxes to float to the top doesn’t matter in Lemma
4.59
. Lastly, we showed that the function respects the defining relations in Lemmas
4.60
,
4.61
,
4.62
, and
4.63
.
∎

Corollary 4.64
.

\mathcal{P}_{0}
is one-dimensional.

Proof.

We have a well-defined map
J:\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}
that sends the empty diagram to 1. So the dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is greater than or equal to 1. On the other hand, this map is an evaluation algorithm, so the dimension of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is at most 1.
∎

Remark
.

This has now shown that the jellyfish algorithm is an invariant on the vector space of closed diagrams for the affine
E_{7}
subfactor planar algebra.

4.9
Minimal Projections in
\mathcal{P}

In this section, we prove that the projections
\emptyset
,
P_{1}=f^{(1)}
,
P_{2}=f^{(2)}
,
P_{3}=f^{(3)}
,
P_{4}=\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S
,
P_{5}=P_{4}\otimes X-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)
,
P_{6}=P_{5}\otimes X-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)
, and
Q_{4}=\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S
are nonisomorphic minimal projections and satisfy the tensor rules for the affine
E_{7}
principal graph.

We define a modified jellyfish algorithm for
\mathcal{P}
that can be used for any box space. The goal of this algorithm is to put the diagrams in a jellyfish form with
S
-boxes connected by at most 2 strands.

Define the
modified jellyfish algorithm
on diagrams in the following way. Let
D\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
be a diagram.

1.

Follow the steps 1 through 6 of the jellyfish algorithm for
\mathcal{P}
except dragging
S
-boxes to the left side of the diagram instead of to the top and keeping the top and bottom boundary points fixed. This may make
D
a sum of diagrams, say
D=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\ell}}a_{i}D_{i}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

2.

Look individually at each
D_{i}
.

(a)

If
D_{i}
has any
S
-box that is cupped, evaluate
D_{i}
to zero.

(b)

If
D_{i}
has no
S
-box that is cupped, then use the
S^{2}
relation to reduce any diagram with an
S^{2}
to a sum of diagrams with at least one less
S
box. Repeat this process until all the resulting summands have adjacent
S
-boxes are connected by at most 3 strands.

(c)

Use equation
16
to write any diagram with two
S
-boxes connected by 3 strands to be a summand of diagrams with at least one less
S
-box. Repeat this process until all the resulting summands have adjacent
S
-boxes connected by at most 2 strands.

3.

Extend the function linearly to be defined on all of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

A diagram where all of its
S
-boxes are on the left side with no strands crossing and each box is connected by at most 2 strands is said to be in
modified jellyfish form
.

Notice that Corollary
4.64
showed that
\emptyset
is a minimal projection.

Lemma 4.65
.

For
n\in\{1,2,3\}
,
\text{Hom}(P_{n},P_{n})
is one-dimensional.

Proof.

Fix
n\in\{1,2,3\}
. Let
f\in\text{Hom}(P_{n},P_{n})
and use the modified jellyfish algorithm on the middle of
f
. Without loss of generality, suppose that the middle of
f
is a diagram in modified jellyfish form. Let
m
be the number of
S
-boxes in
f
in this form. Let
S_{1}
be the
S
-box at the top left.
S_{1}
can have at most 3 strands on the top boundary, depending on the value of
n
. If
m\geq 2
, then
S_{1}
can have at most 2 strands connected to the
S
-box below it, say
S_{2}
. It then has at least 3 more strands to connect to either the bottom or another
S
-box.

Say a strand of
S_{1}
connects to another
S
-box, say
S_{k}
. Then between
S_{1}
and
S_{k}
there must be some
S^{2}
since there is a closed region and there are no crossing of strands. This contradicts that each of the
S
-boxes are connected by at most 2 strands. A similar argument shows that the strands of
S_{1}
cannot connect to the bottom boundary. Therefore,
m<2
.

Suppose
m=1
. As there are eight strands of
S
and six points on the boundary,
S
must have a cap, so
f=0
. Suppose
m=0
. Then as
f\in\mathcal{TL}
and
P_{1}=f^{(1)}
,
P_{2}=f^{(2)}
, and
P_{3}=f^{(3)}
, we know from Temperley-Lieb being a subfactor planar algebra that
f
must be a scalar multiple of the projection. Therefore, the dimension of
\text{Hom}(P_{n},P_{n})
is at most 1. On the other hand,
\text{id}_{P_{n}}=P_{n}\in\text{Hom}(P_{n},P_{n})
. The trace of
P_{n}
is nonzero and the 0-box space is one-dimensional, so
P_{n}\neq 0
. Therefore, the
\text{Hom}(P_{n},P_{n})
is one-dimensional.
∎

The below calculations will become useful later on.

Lemma 4.66
.

The following equalities hold in
\mathcal{P}
:

(i)

P_{4}S=SP_{4}=-2P_{4}
,

(ii)

Q_{4}S=SQ_{4}=3Q_{4}
,

(iii)

P_{5}(S\otimes X)=(S\otimes X)P_{5}=-2P_{5}
, and

(iv)

P_{6}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=-2P_{6}
.

Proof.

For part (i) we use the
S^{2}
relation as well as that
S
absorbs
f^{(4)}
to see that

\displaystyle P_{4}S

\displaystyle=(\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}S-\frac{1}{5}S^{2})=\frac{3}{5}S-\frac{1}{5}(S+6f^{(4)})=\frac{2}{5}S-\frac{6}{5}f^{(4)}=-2(\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S)=-2P_{4}.

By symmetry, we also have
SP_{4}=-2P_{4}
.

For part (ii) we use similar reasoning to get that

\displaystyle Q_{4}S=SQ_{4}=(\frac{2}{5}S+\frac{1}{5}S^{2})=\frac{2}{5}S+\frac{1}{5}(S+6f^{(4)})=\frac{3}{5}S+\frac{6}{5}f^{(4)}=3(\frac{1}{5}S+\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)})=3Q_{4},

as desired.

For part (iii), we use part (i):

\displaystyle P_{5}(S\otimes X)

\displaystyle=(P_{4}\otimes X)(S\otimes X)-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)(S\otimes X)=-2(P_{4}\otimes X)+\frac{4}{3}\cdot 2(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)=-2P_{5}.

Similarly,
(S\otimes X)P_{5}=-2P_{5}
. For part (iv), we use part (iii):

\displaystyle P_{6}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=(P_{5}\otimes X)(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})=-2(P_{5}\otimes X)+\frac{3}{2}\cdot 2(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)=-2P_{6}.

Similarly,
(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})P_{6}=-2P_{6}
.
∎

Lemma 4.67
.

Both
\text{Hom}(P_{4},P_{4})
and
\text{Hom}(Q_{4},Q_{4})
are one-dimensional.

Proof.

Let
f
be in either
\text{Hom}(P_{4},P_{4})
or
\text{Hom}(Q_{4},Q_{4})
and without loss of generality, suppose
f
is a diagram whose middle is in modified jellyfish form. Label the
S
-boxes on the left, starting with the top:
S_{1},...S_{m}
.

Suppose
S_{1}
connects to the top boundary by
1\leq p\leq 4
strands and that
m\geq 2
. Since
f
is in modified jellyfish form,
S_{1}
connects to
S_{2}
by at most 2 strands. Then
S_{1}
has at least two other strands to connect somewhere. However, as done in the previous lemma, this will block off the other
S
-boxes and require somewhere to have an
S^{2}
, which cannot happen. Thus
m=1
or
0
.

Suppose
m=1
. If
f
is not zero,
S
has no cap so has all of the strands are on the boundary. Thus the rest of
f
is in Temperley-Lieb which can be evaluated to a scalar, say
a\in\mathbb{C}
. So the middle of
f
is
aS
. That is,
f=aP_{4}SP_{4}
, or
f=aQ_{4}SQ_{4}
. Thus, it is enough to calculate
P_{4}SP_{4}
and
Q_{4}SQ_{4}
in this case. Using Lemma
4.66
, we get

\displaystyle P_{4}SP_{4}=-2P_{4}^{2}=-2P_{4}.

Similarly, we also get

Q_{4}SQ_{4}=3Q_{4}^{2}=3Q_{4}.

So in either case
f
is a multiple of the projection.

If
m=0
then the middle of
f
is in Temperley-Lieb.
P_{4}
and
Q_{4}
are uncuppable and uncappable, so the only nonzero option would be the middle of
f
to be straight strands. Thus
f
is a scalar multiple of the projection. Thus the hom spaces are at most one-dimensional. The traces of
P_{4}
and
Q_{4}
are both nonzero, so the hom spaces are one-dimensional.
∎

Lemma 4.68
.

The dimension of
\text{Hom}(P_{5},P_{5})
is 1.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
f
be a diagram whose middle is in modified jellyfish form. Enumerate the
S
-boxes on the left side:
S_{1}
, …,
S_{m}
.

Suppose
m\geq 2
. Then
S_{1}
connects to the top by at most 5 strands and to
S_{2}
by at most 2 strands, leaving at least 1 strand that will need to pass by
S_{2}
. If the strand connects to another
S
-box, say
S_{k}
, then between
S_{1}
and
S_{k}
there will be an
S^{2}
, which would contradict
f
being in modified jellyfish form.

Assume
m=1
. Then, up to some scalar multiple, we must have
f=P_{5}(S\otimes X)P_{5}=-2P_{5}
, by Lemma
4.66
.

Assume
m=0
. Then
f
is in Temperley-Lieb. Since
P_{5}
is uncuppable and uncappable, the only option is for the middle of
f
to be a scalar multiple of straight strands. Thus,
f=aP_{5}^{2}=P_{5}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
. Since the trace of
P_{5}
is nonzero, the dimension of
\text{Hom}(P_{5},P_{5})=1
.
∎

Lemma 4.69
.

The dimension of
\text{Hom}(P_{6},P_{6})
is
1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
f
be a diagram whose middle is in modified jellyfish form. Enumerate the
S
-boxes from top to bottom:
S_{1}
, … ,
S_{m}
.

Suppose
m\geq 2
.
S_{1}
connects to the top by at most
6
strands. Suppose
S_{1}
connects to the top by at most 5 strands. Then the same argument as in Lemma
4.68
gives a contradiction. Suppose
S_{1}
connects to the top by 6 strands. Then since
P_{6}
absorbs
S
,
P_{6}
will be capped, resulting in
f=0
.

Let
m=1
. Then, up to scalar multiple,
f=P_{6}(S\otimes X^{\otimes 2})P_{6}=-2P_{6}^{2}=-2P_{6}
.

Finally, suppose
m=0
. Then, as
P_{6}
is uncappable and uncuppable, the middle of
f
is a scalar multiple straight strands, so
f=aP_{6}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
. Therefore,
\text{Hom}(P_{6},P_{6})
is at most one-dimensional. Since the trace of
P_{6}
is nonzero, the hom space is one-dimensional.
∎

Thus we have proved that
P_{0}=\emptyset
,
P_{1}
, … ,
P_{6}
, and
Q_{4}
are all minimal projections. We now show that they are all nonisomorphic.

Lemma 4.70
.

The dimension of
\text{Hom}(P_{i},P_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j
,
i,j\in\{0,...,6\}
(and
P_{0}\coloneq\emptyset
).

Proof.

Fix
0\leq i<j\leq 6
. Without loss of generality, suppose
f\in\text{Hom}(P_{i},P_{j})
is a diagram whose middle is in modified jellyfish form. Enumerate the
S
-boxes starting with the top as
S_{1}
, …,
S_{m}
.

Suppose
m\geq 2
. As
j\leq 6
,
S_{1}
is connected by at most 6 strands at the top. Since
P_{6}
and
P_{5}
absorbs
S
, if
S_{1}
is connected by more than 4 strands then
f=0
. If
S_{1}
is connected by 4 or less strands at the top then it is connected by at most 2 strands to
S_{2}
, leaving at least 2 strands that must go to the bottom boundary or another
S
-box. As seen before, this will force there to be some
S^{2}
which contradicts the middle of
f
being in modified jellyfish form.

Suppose
m=1
. Then
i+j\geq 8
since all the strands of
S
must be on the boundary. Thus, we have
P_{j}
is either
P_{5}
or
P_{6}
. Both
P_{5}
and
P_{6}
absorb
S
, so the middle of
f
can then be recognized to be in Temperley-Lieb. We then consider the final case.

Supppose
m=0
. Then the middle of
f
is in Temperley-Lieb. Since the number of boundary points on the top and bottom are not equal, there must be a cap or cup. However, all
P_{i},P_{j}
are uncuppable and uncappable, so
f=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.71
.

For all
0\leq i\leq 6
, the dimension of
\text{Hom}(Q_{4},P_{i})=0
.

Proof.

By substituting
Q_{4}
for
P_{4}
in the previous lemma, we get the result for
i\neq 4
. Let
f\in\text{Hom}(Q_{4},P_{4})
be a diagram whose middle is in modified jellyfish form. Since there are exactly 4 points on the top and bottom, using similar reasoning to the previous lemmas, the only option is that
f
is a scalar multiple of
Q_{4}SP_{4}=3Q_{4}P_{4}
(when the middle of
f
has one
S
-box) or
Q_{4}P_{4}
(when the middle of
f
is in Temperley-Lieb). However,

\displaystyle Q_{4}P_{4}

\displaystyle=(\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S)(\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S)=\frac{6}{25}f^{(4)}+\frac{3}{25}S-\frac{2}{25}S-\frac{1}{25}S^{2}=\frac{6}{25}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{25}S-\frac{1}{25}(S+6f^{(4)})=0.

∎

In this section we have now proved the following.

Corollary 4.72
.

The projections
\emptyset
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
,
P_{3}
,
P_{4}
,
P_{5}
,
P_{6}
, and
Q_{4}
are all minimal and nonisomorpic.

Next we show that the minimal projections of
\mathcal{P}
found in the previous corollary satisfy the properties to have the affine
E_{7}
Dynkin diagram as its principal graph.

Lemma 4.73
.

In
\mathcal{P}
, the minimal projections satisfy the principal graph given in (
4
).

Proof.

Notice that
\emptyset\otimes X\cong P_{1}
,
P_{1}\otimes X\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}
,
P_{2}\otimes X\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}
,
P_{3}\otimes X\cong P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}
,
P_{4}\otimes X\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
, and
P_{5}\otimes X\cong P_{4}\oplus P_{6}
were all shown in Lemma
4.9
. What is left to show is that
Q_{4}\otimes X\cong P_{3}
and
P_{6}\otimes X\cong P_{5}
.

In Lemma
4.26
, we found that
Q_{4}\otimes X=2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)
. Define
f=\sqrt{2}(Q_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})
and
f^{*}=\sqrt{2}(X^{\otimes 3}\text{ev}_{X})(Q_{4}\otimes X)
. Then
ff^{*}=2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)=Q_{4}\otimes X
and
f^{*}f=2E_{4}(Q_{4})=2\cdot\frac{1}{2}f^{(3)}=P_{3}
. Thus
Q_{4}\otimes X\cong P_{3}
.

Similarly, in Lemma
4.29
, we proved that
P_{6}\otimes X=2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)
. Let
g=\sqrt{2}(P_{6}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 5}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})
and
g^{*}=\sqrt{2}(X^{\otimes 5}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{6}\otimes X)
. Then
gg^{*}=2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)=P_{6}\otimes X
and
g^{*}g=2E_{6}(P_{6})=2\cdot\frac{1}{2}P_{5}=P_{5}
. Therefore,
P_{6}\otimes X\cong P_{5}
, which completes the proof.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of the main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In Corollary
4.64
we show that the space of closed diagrams in one-dimensional. To show
\mathcal{P}
is spherical it suffices to show
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\text{Hom}(X,X)=\text{span}\{X\}
. However, this is clear as
X=f^{(1)}
is a minimal projection.

To show positive-definiteness, we employ section 4.2 of
[
20
]
. In that section, they present an explicit positive orthogonal basis of the box spaces for any unshaded planar algebra whose corresponding category of projections is semisimple, with all minimal projections having positive trace. Then they conclude in Theorem 4.18 that their presentation results in a positive definite planar algebra. Since we already know the principal graph of
\mathcal{P}
is affine
E_{7}
from Lemma
4.73
and Lemma
4.6
gives that all the minimal projections have positive trace, we obtain that
\mathcal{P}
is positive definite. Now that we have an explicit basis, we get that
\mathcal{P}
is evaluable.
∎

5. Necessary Relations for Affine
E_{7}
Unshaded Subfactor Planar Algebras

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the (unshaded) subfactor planar algebra with principal graph
\tilde{E}_{7}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{P}}
be the category created from
\mathcal{P}
. Define
X
to be a single, black, unoriented strand. Let
\mathcal{P}
have a principal graph with the following labeling:

[\emptyset]
[P_{1}]
[P_{2}]
[Q_{4}]
[P_{3}]
[P_{4}]
[P_{5}]
[P_{6}]

(40)

Lemma 5.1
.

\mathcal{P}
has index 4. Further, the traces of all the minimal projections are:

(i)

\text{tr}(\emptyset)=1
,

(ii)

\text{tr}(P_{1})=2
,

(iii)

\text{tr}(P_{2})=3
,

(iv)

\text{tr}(P_{3})=4
,

(v)

\text{tr}(Q_{4})=2
,

(vi)

\text{tr}(P_{4})=3
,

(vii)

\text{tr}(P_{5})=2
, and

(viii)

\text{tr}(P_{6})=1
.

Proof.

By assumption
\text{tr}(\emptyset)=1
. Choose
\delta=2
and the map
tr
from the Perron-Frobenius theorem to work as stated in the lemma. In this case, we can compute the traces of the other minimal projections as follows:

\displaystyle 2\text{tr}(\emptyset)=\text{tr}(P_{1})=2,

\displaystyle 2\text{tr}(P_{1})-\text{tr}(\emptyset)=\text{tr}(P_{2})=3,

\displaystyle 2\text{tr}(P_{2})-\text{tr}(P_{1})=\text{tr}(P_{3})=4,

\displaystyle\text{tr}(Q_{4})=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}(P_{3})=2.

Further,

\displaystyle 2\text{tr}(P_{3})-\text{tr}(Q_{4})-\text{tr}(P_{2})=\text{tr}(P_{4})=3,

\displaystyle 2\text{tr}(P_{4})-\text{tr}(P_{3})=\text{tr}(P_{5})=2,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\text{tr}(P_{6})=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}(P_{5})=1.

Since the trace formula holds for all vertices, by Perron-Frobenius these values must be correct.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

The following isomorphisms hold in
\mathcal{P}
:

(i)

P_{1}\cong f^{(1)}=X
,

(ii)

P_{2}\cong f^{(2)}
, and

(iii)

P_{3}\cong f^{(3)}
.

Proof.

By the principal graph and Wenzl’s relation,
\emptyset\otimes X\cong P_{1}\cong f^{(1)}
. Similarly,
P_{1}\otimes X\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\cong f^{(0)}\oplus f^{(2)}
, which gives that
f^{(2)}\cong P_{2}
. Using the same method,
P_{2}\otimes X\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\cong f^{(1)}\cong f^{(3)}
, so
f^{(3)}\cong P_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

X^{\otimes 4}\cong\emptyset\oplus\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}
.

Proof.

Recall the result given by Jones in Proposition
2.1
. To compute
X^{\otimes 4}
it is enough to find all endpoints of a length
4
path starting at
\emptyset
. The lemma is then immediate.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists no representatives of
Q_{4},P_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{m}
where
0\leq m\leq 3
. Further, there exists a unique representative of
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
such that
Q_{4}+P_{4}=f^{(4)}
.

Proof.

Suppose
Q_{4}^{\prime}
(respectively,
P_{4}^{\prime}
) is a representative of
Q_{4}
(respectively,
P_{4}
) in
\mathcal{P}_{m}
where
0\leq m\leq 3
. However, by Proposition
2.1
,
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
cannot be in the tensor decomposition of
X^{\otimes m}
since they are both distance 4 away from
\emptyset
. Thus
\text{dim}(\text{Hom}(X^{\otimes m},Q_{4}))=\text{dim}(\text{Hom}(X^{\otimes m},P_{4}))=0
, so
Q_{4}^{\prime}=P_{4}^{\prime}=0
, a contradiction.

The rest of this proof is nearly identical to Lemma 3.2 of
[
17
]
so will be omitted.
∎

Remark
.

From now on, define
P_{4}
and
Q_{4}
to be the representatives found in the previous lemma. In particular,
P_{4},Q_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
and
f^{(4)}=P_{4}+Q_{4}
.

Definition 5.1
.

(Generator of the affine
E_{7}
Subfactor Planar Algebra)

\mathcal{P}
with the principal graph in figure
40
has a special element
S\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
:

\begin{overpic}[fig/box.png]
\put(3.6,6.2){$S$}
\put(4.2,14.0){$...$}
\put(4.0,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}

where
S\coloneq 3Q_{4}-2P_{4}
.

We will prove at the end of this section that
S
is indeed a generator of this planar algebra. In the work that follows, we will prove that if
\mathcal{P}
is a subfactor planar algebra with affine
E_{7}
principal graph, then
\mathcal{P}
will have the following relations. (Notice we already proved relation (i) since
\mathcal{P}
has index 4.)

Definition 5.2
.

(Relations of the affine
E_{7}
Subfactor Planar Algebra) Relations of the
\tilde{E}_{7}
subfactor planar algebra include:

(i)

(
the bubble relation
)
