The rest of the section is devoted to proving the following theorem.

Theorem 5.5
.

If
\mathcal{P}
is an unshaded subfactor planar algebra with principal graph
\tilde{E}_{7}
with labeling given in figure
40
, then
\mathcal{P}
is generated by the element
S
given in Definition
5.1
with relations given in Definition
5.2
.

Lemma 5.6
.

We have the leaf relations
Q_{4}\otimes X=2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)
and
P_{6}\otimes X=2(P_{6}\otimes X)e_{6}(P_{6}\otimes X)
.

Proof.

This follows from
Q_{4}
and
P_{6}
being leaves in the principal graph and Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
are uncuppable and uncappable.

Proof.

Since
f^{(4)}=Q_{4}+P_{4}
, we obtain that
f^{(4)}Q_{4}=Q_{4}^{2}+P_{4}Q_{4}
and
f^{(4)}P_{4}=Q_{4}P_{4}+P_{4}^{2}
. Using that
P_{4}
and
Q_{4}
are nonisomorphic minimal projections we obtain that
f^{(4)}Q_{4}=Q_{4}
and
f^{(4)}P_{4}=P_{4}
. A symmetric proof proves that
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
are also uncuppable.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

We can choose the following representatives for the minimal projections:

(i)

Q_{4}\coloneq\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S
,

(ii)

P_{4}\coloneq\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S
,

(iii)

P_{5}\coloneq P_{4}\otimes X-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)
, and

(iv)

P_{6}\coloneq P_{5}\otimes X-\frac{3}{2}(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)
.

Proof.

Since
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
defined in the lemma are in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
it is enough for parts (i) and (ii) to show that
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
are identical to the representatives chosen from Lemma
5.4
. Using that
f^{(4)}=Q_{4}+P_{4}
and
S=3Q_{4}-2P_{4}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{5}(Q_{4}+P_{4})+\frac{1}{5}(3Q_{4}-2P_{4})=Q_{4},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{3}{5}f^{(4)}-\frac{1}{5}S

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{5}(P_{4}+Q_{4})-\frac{1}{5}(3Q_{2}-2P_{4})=P_{4}.

By the principal graph,
P_{4}\otimes X\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
. Therefore, there exists an
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that
P_{5}=P_{4}\otimes X-a(P_{4}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{3}(X^{\otimes 3}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{4}\otimes X)
. Since
P_{3}=f^{(3)}
and
P_{4}
is uncuppable and uncappable,
P_{5}=P_{4}\otimes X-a(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)
. Taking the trace gives that
\text{tr}(P_{5})=2=2\text{tr}(P_{4})-a\text{tr}(P_{4})=(2-a)\cdot 3
so
a=\frac{4}{3}
.

Before proving part (iv), notice that
P_{5}(P_{4}\otimes X)=(P_{4}\otimes X)^{2}-\frac{4}{3}(P_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(P_{4}\otimes X)^{2}=P_{5}
since
P_{4}^{2}=P_{4}
. Then, using the principal graph, there exists a
b\in\mathbb{C}
such that
P_{6}=P_{5}\otimes X-b(P_{5}\otimes X)(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{coev}_{X})P_{4}(X^{\otimes 4}\otimes\text{ev}_{X})(P_{5}\otimes X)=P_{5}\otimes X-b(P_{5}\otimes X)e_{5}(P_{5}\otimes X)
. Taking the trace gives
b=\frac{3}{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
S^{2}=6f^{(4)}+S
.

Proof.

Since
Q_{4}
and
P_{4}
are nonisomorphic minimal projections,
Q_{4}^{2}=Q_{4}
,
P_{4}^{2}=P_{4}
, and
Q_{4}P_{4}=P_{4}Q_{4}=0
. Thus

\displaystyle S^{2}=(3Q_{4}-2P_{4})^{2}=9Q_{4}+4P_{4}=6(Q_{4}+P_{4})+(3Q_{4}-2P_{4})=6f^{(4)}+S

as we claimed.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

S
is self-adjoint.

Proof.

S
is a linear combination of projections, so the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))f^{(10)}=0
.

Proof.

If the dimension of
\text{Hom}(f^{(10)},\emptyset)=0
then the lemma is true. We then focus on proving this. Recall that
f^{(1)}\cong P_{1}
,
f^{(2)}\cong P_{2}
,
f^{(3)}\cong P_{3}
, and
f^{(4)}\cong P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}
. Wenzl’s relation and the principal graph gives

\displaystyle f^{(4)}\otimes X\cong(P_{4}\oplus Q_{4})\otimes X\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\oplus P_{3}\cong f^{(3)}\oplus f^{(5)}

so
f^{(5)}\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle f^{(5)}\otimes X\cong(P_{3}\oplus P_{5})\otimes X\cong P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{6}\cong f^{(4)}\oplus f^{(6)}\cong P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus f^{(6)},

which gives that
f^{(6)}\cong P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{6}
. Continuing this process, we get

\displaystyle f^{(6)}\otimes X\cong(P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{6})\otimes X\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\oplus P_{5}\cong f^{(5)}\oplus f^{(7)}\cong P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\oplus f^{(7)}

so
f^{(7)}\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{5}
. Next, we find that

\displaystyle f^{(7)}\otimes X\cong(P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{5})\otimes X\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{6}\cong f^{(6)}\oplus f^{(8)}\cong P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus P_{6}\oplus f^{(8)}

giving that
f^{(8)}\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle f^{(8)}\otimes X\cong(\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4})\otimes X\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\cong f^{(7)}\oplus f^{(9)}\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{5}\oplus f^{(9)}

which gives that
f^{(9)}\cong P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{3}
.

Finally,

\displaystyle f^{(9)}\otimes X\cong(P_{1}\oplus P_{3}\oplus P_{3})\otimes X\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}\cong f^{(8)}\oplus f^{(10)}\cong\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}\oplus f^{(10)}

Therefore
f^{(10)}\cong P_{2}\oplus P_{2}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus P_{4}
, which gives the result.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

In
\mathcal{P}
we have the following partial traces.

(i)

E_{4}(P_{4})=E_{4}^{\ell}(P_{4})=\frac{3}{4}P_{3}
, and

(ii)

E_{4}(Q_{4})=E_{4}^{\ell}(Q_{4})=\frac{1}{2}P_{3}
.

Proof.

Since
P_{4}
is uncuppable and uncappable, so is
E_{4}(P_{4})
. Thus we recognize that
E_{4}(P_{4})\in\text{Hom}(f^{(3)},f^{(3)})
since
E_{4}(P_{4})=f^{(3)}E_{4}(P_{4})f^{(3)}
. Since
f^{(3)}
is a minimal projection, there exists an
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that
E_{4}(P_{4})=af^{(3)}
. Taking the trace of both sides gives that
a=\frac{3}{4}
. Similarly, there exists a
b\in\mathbb{C}
such that
E_{4}^{\ell}(P_{4})=f^{(3)}E_{4}^{\ell}(P_{4})f^{(3)}=bf^{(3)}
. Take the trace of both
E_{4}^{\ell}(P_{4})
and
f^{(3)}
. Use sphericality to notice that
\text{tr}(E_{4}^{\ell}(P_{4}))=\text{tr}(P_{4})=3
. Therefore,
b=\frac{3}{4}
.

We do nearly the same argument for
Q_{4}
to get part (ii).
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

S
is U.C.C.S.

Proof.

S
is a linear combination of uncuppable and uncappable elements so
S
itself is uncuppable and uncappable. Further,

\displaystyle E_{4}(S)=3E_{4}(Q_{4})-2E_{4}(P_{4})=3\cdot\frac{1}{2}P_{3}-2\cdot\frac{3}{4}P_{3}=0

and similarly,
E_{4}^{\ell}(S)=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

S
is nonzero.

Proof.

Since
S
is U.C.C.S.,
\text{tr}(S)=0
. So,
\text{tr}(S^{2})=6\text{tr}(f^{(4)})+\text{tr}(S)=6\cdot 5=30
. Thus
S^{2}
is nonzero and so is
S
.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

There exists an
a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
\mathcal{F}(S)=aS
.

Proof.

Since
S
is U.C.C.S.,
\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}=\mathcal{R}(S)\neq 0
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))f^{(8)}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))
. Recall in Lemma
5.11
that
f^{(8)}=\emptyset\oplus P_{2}\oplus P_{4}\oplus Q_{4}
, so the dimension of
\text{Hom}(f^{(8)},\emptyset)
is
1
. Therefore, there exists a scalar
a\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}=\mathcal{R}(S)=a\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))f^{(8)}=a\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))
. Since
S
is nonzero, so is
a
.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

In
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{F}(S)=S
.

Proof.

Recall that
Q_{4}\otimes X=2(Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)
. We first take the left partial trace of the leaf property:
E_{5}^{\ell}(Q_{4}\otimes X)=2E_{5}^{\ell}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
. Then we expand out using
Q_{4}=\frac{2}{5}f^{(4)}+\frac{1}{5}S
to obtain on the left-hand side:

\displaystyle E_{5}^{\ell}(Q_{4}\otimes X)=\frac{2}{5}E_{5}^{\ell}(f^{(4)}\otimes X)+\frac{1}{5}E_{5}^{\ell}(S\otimes X)=\frac{2}{5}E_{5}^{\ell}(f^{(4)}\otimes X)=\frac{2}{5}(E_{4}^{\ell}(f^{(4)})\otimes X)

since
S
is U.C.C.S. We also obtain
2E_{5}^{\ell}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
equals

\displaystyle\frac{8}{25}E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X))+\frac{4}{25}E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X))+\frac{4}{25}E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X))+\frac{2}{25}E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X)).

Now we rainbow the expanded versions of
E_{5}^{\ell}(Q_{4}\otimes X)
and
2E_{5}^{\ell}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X))
onto
f^{(8)}
and obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}(Q_{4}\otimes X))f^{(8)}=\frac{2}{5}(E_{4}^{\ell}(f^{(4)})\otimes X)f^{(8)}=0

since
f^{(8)}
is uncappable. We compute
2\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
term-by-term. First, we use Wenzl’s relation to get that
\frac{8}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
equals

\displaystyle\frac{8}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}-\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{6}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{6}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}

where the first equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncappable and the second equality comes from
f^{(4)}
being uncuppable and uncappable. We again use Wenzl’s relation and obtain
-\frac{6}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}
equals

\displaystyle-\frac{6}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}-\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}.

where the first equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the second equality comes from
f^{(4)}
being uncuppable and uncappable. Expanding
f^{(2)}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}e_{1}))f^{(8)})=0

where the second equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable.

The second term,
\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(4)}\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
, expands to

\displaystyle\frac{4}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}-\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

where the first equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the second equality comes from
S
being uncuppable and uncappable. Expanding
f^{(3)}
in
-\frac{3}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
gives

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}-\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

where the first equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the second equality comes from
S
being uncuppable and uncappable. Expanding
f^{(2)}
in
\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{2}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((X^{\otimes 5})e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}(e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}(e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}),

where the equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable. This equals

-\frac{1}{25}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}(S))f^{(8)}=-\frac{a}{25}\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}=-\frac{a}{25}\mathcal{R}(S),

since
S
is U.C.C.S.

The third term,
\frac{4}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(4)}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
, becomes

\displaystyle\frac{4}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}-\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}

where the first equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the second equality comes from
S
being uncuppable and uncappable. Then
-\frac{3}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(3)}\otimes X^{\otimes 2})))f^{(8)}
equals

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}-\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}

where the second equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the third equality comes from
S
being uncuppable and uncappable. Finally, expanding
f^{(2)}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(f^{(2)}\otimes X^{\otimes 3})))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}(\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}(X^{\otimes 5})))f^{(8)}-\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}e_{1}))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}e_{3}e_{2}e_{1}))f^{(8)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{25}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(S))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a^{-1}}{25}\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a^{-1}}{25}\mathcal{R}(S),

where the second equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable and the fifth equality comes from
S
being U.C.C.S.

The fourth and final term expands out in the following way

\displaystyle\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((S\otimes X)e_{4}(S\otimes X)))f^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(S^{2})f^{(8)}=\frac{2}{25}(\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}+\mathcal{R}(f^{(4)})f^{(8)})=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(S)f^{(8)}=\frac{2}{25}\mathcal{R}(S),

where the first equality comes from clicking the top
S
-box counterclockwise and the bottom
S
-box clockwise, the third equality comes from
f^{(8)}
being uncuppable and uncappable, and the fourth equality comes from
S
being U.C.C.S.

Therefore,
\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}(Q_{4}\otimes X)=2\mathcal{R}(E_{5}^{\ell}((Q_{4}\otimes X)e_{4}(Q_{4}\otimes X)))f^{(8)}
is equivalent to
0=(-\frac{a}{25}-\frac{a^{-1}}{25}+\frac{2}{25})\mathcal{R}(S)
. Since
\mathcal{R}(S)\neq 0
this gives
a=1
.
∎

Lemma 5.17
.

\mathcal{P}
satisfies the jellyfish relation of Definition
5.2
.

Proof.

Define the below diagram to be
T
.

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithbelowcrossings.png]
\put(7.7,12.5){$S$}
\put(16.1,2.0){$...$}
\put(8.3,2.0){$...$}
\end{overpic}

Recall in Lemma
5.11
we showed
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))f^{(8)}=0
. The same proof gives that
Tf^{(10)}=0
. We can expand
f^{(10)}
in both
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))f^{(8)}
and
Tf^{(10)}
. The same terms in
f^{(10)}
will cap
S
and become zero. The nonzero terms will be the identity on 10 strands and any element with a cup on the first two or last two strands.

Suppose
T
is multiplied by an element in the expansion of
f^{(10)}
that has a cup on the first two strands. Recall, from Lemma
4.11
and Lemma
4.12
, that

\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottomtwist.png]
\put(7.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(7)$}}
\put(7.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}
=-i
\begin{overpic}[fig/boxwithovercrossingonbottom2.png]
\put(7.0,-2.0){\tiny{$(7)$}}
\put(7.0,2.0){\tiny{$...$}}
\put(5.0,17.0){$S$}
\end{overpic}
=\mathcal{R}(S)
.

Meanwhile, a cup on the first two strands of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{R}(S))=\mathcal{R}(S)
. Similarly, we can see that a cup on the last two strands for
T
and
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))
both equal
\mathcal{R}(S)
. Therefore, when expanding
f^{(10)}
for both
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))f^{(10)}
and
Tf^{(10)}
we get that the terms with cups between the first two or last two strands are equal. Therefore, all the terms in the expansion of
f^{(10)}
have been shown to be equal except the identity on ten strands. Since
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))f^{(10)}=Tf^{(10)}=0
, this gives that the
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))X^{\otimes 10}=TX^{\otimes 10}
. Thus
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}(S))=T
, as we wished.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

In Lemma
5.1
we showed the bubble relation. In Lemma
5.13
we showed that
S
is U.C.C.S. In Lemma
5.9
we showed that
S^{2}=6f^{(4)}+S
. In Lemma
5.17
we showed the jellyfish relation.

What is left to show is that
S
generates
\mathcal{P}
. We know that
S
generates
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, a subfactor planar algebra with principal graph
\tilde{E}_{7}
, as defined in Theorem
4.1
. Therefore, it is enough to show that the dimension of all the box-spaces are equal. However, this is clear as a positive definite basis of each box-space created in
[
20
]
relies only on the principal graph and the traces of the minimal projections. Since the principal graphs and the trace of minimal projections are equal for
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we get that
S
must generate
\mathcal{P}
.
∎

Corollary 5.18
.

There is exactly one unshaded subfactor planar algebra with principal graph
\tilde{E}_{7}
.

Proof.

We have at least one by Theorem
4.1
. Further, if we have a subfactor planar algebra with principal graph
\tilde{E}_{7}
, Theorem
5.5
showed it must have the same presentation as the one found in Theorem
4.1
.
∎
